<margin>y 4</margin>

# BASILIUS VALENTINUS
# FRIAR
->Of the Order of<-
## St. BENEDICT
->His Last<-
# WILL and TESTAMENT.

->Which<-

->He himself, being alone, hid under a marble table behind the High Altar of the Cathedral Church, in the Imperial City of Erfurt <note>original: "Erford"</note>: leaving it there to be found by him whom God’s Providence should make worthy of it.<-

->*Wherein he sufficiently declares the methods he used to obtain the Philosopher's Stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" was a legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and serving as an elixir of life.</note>, and taught them also to his fellow monks <note>original: "Collegians"</note>, all of whom also succeeded in obtaining the Philosopher's Stone: by which not only the leprous bodies of the impure and inferior metals are transformed <note>original: "reduced"</note> into the pure and perfect body of Gold and Silver, but also all manner of diseases whatsoever are cured in the bodies of unhealthy men, and kept thereby in perfect health even to the prolonging of their lives.*<-

---

->LONDON,<-
->Printed in the Year of our Lord <note>original: "Anno Domini"</note>, 1657.<-

<summary>This is the title page for the "Last Will and Testament" of Basilius Valentinus, a legendary Benedictine monk and alchemist. It claims the manuscript was hidden behind an altar in Erfurt and contains the secret for creating the Philosopher's Stone to transmute metals and heal diseases.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Erfurt, Transmutation, Medicine, Benedictine Order, 17th-century printing</keywords>

## TO THE
# R E A D E R .

<image-desc>A large, square decorative initial 'T' embellished with intricate floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>

This book is full of mysteries; if you wish to have them unlocked, then endeavor to obtain the key which the author used. For his humanity during his life was in harmony with the Divine. As soon as he obtained a truth regarding these mysteries, he immediately fell to his knees, returning thanks to the Creator for the light he received. And when he acquired any true medicines, he set to work, curing

# TO THE READER

This book is full of mysteries <note>original: "myſteries"; here referring to hidden spiritual or alchemical secrets</note>. If you wish to have them unlocked, then endeavor to obtain the key which the author used. For the humanity of his life was in harmony with the Divine. As soon as he obtained a truth regarding these mysteries, he immediately fell to his knees, returning thanks to the Creator for the light he received. And when he acquired any true medicines, he went to work, curing

<summary>The preface advises the reader that the book's secrets can only be understood by adopting the author's own pious and grateful mindset. It highlights the author's practice of combining spiritual devotion with the practical application of medical knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>mysteries, divine harmony, gratitude, prayer, alchemy, medicine, spiritual practice</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory address to the reader, emphasizing the charitable nature of the author's medical practice and warning the unworthy to stay away from the text.</meta>

## To the Reader.

He provided his services for free, even for those diseases which human learning <note>original: "humane learning"</note> judged to be incurable; and if he acquired any riches, he used them to supply the needs of the poor.

Let not those with wicked <note>original: "vitious"</note> hearts and hands presume to meddle with this book, lest instead of a blessing they should meet with a curse. Therefore,

->Away from here, stay far away, you who are unholy. <note>original Latin: "Procul hinc, procul ite prophani." A common classical formula warning the uninitiated or unworthy to stay away from sacred mysteries.</note><-

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the Table of Contents for the following treatise, outlining the foundational principles of mineralogy, alchemy, and the medicinal use of metals.</meta>

# In this book are contained
## these following heads.

1. The nature, condition, and property of mine-works <note>original: "Myne-works"; referring to the natural environment where minerals are found</note> in which minerals and metals grow; of their first seed, birth, quality, and property, and of their exhalation and inhalation <note>The theory that minerals "breathe" or release vapors within the earth</note>, and how they are supported in their growth and augmentation.

2. The origin of ores <note>original: "Oars"</note>, stones, rocks, and marbles; of their passages and clefts, their liquids, juices, energies, and operations—namely of gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, and of minerals.

3. A plain, literal explanation of that Universal Elixir <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, whereby a healthful life is prolonged, all diseases expelled, and abundance of riches obtained; with a perfect declaration of the Twelve Keys <note>A famous alchemical text attributed to Basilius Valentinus</note>.

4. A particular description of metals, whereby their several endowments are declared, and how they serve to keep man in perfect health. This includes many manuals for working with them to help man live more comfortably in outward means, and how metals and minerals in particular are brought to their highest preparation, by which it is ensured that men may live in this world without lacking necessary things.

<summary>This table of contents outlines a comprehensive guide to the natural formation of minerals, the chemical properties of various metals, and the alchemical secrets of the Universal Elixir and the Twelve Keys.</summary>

<keywords>mineralogy, alchemy, Universal Elixir, Twelve Keys, metallurgy, medicinal metals, mining, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
5. A transcendent, most precious, and wonderful medicine of metals and minerals, and of other things which the Creator of heaven and earth, and all the things contained therein, ordained for the good of man.

# PREFACE
### AND
# ENTRANCE
### UPON
## BASILIUS VALENTINUS
### *His last*
# TESTAMENT.

---

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'W' set within a square frame featuring floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>

*Whereas the time has come, that by the good pleasure of Almighty God my Creator, and his dearly beloved Son* JESUS CHRIST, *my laboratory <note>original: "Laboratorie"; here referring both to his physical workspace and his earthly life of work</note> in this corruptible world draws to an end, and I am to approach from this earthly laboratory unto the heavenly. I am creeping daily and hourly nearer to the end, and am to close my life according to the appointed time of my dear Savior, aiming at the true Manna <note>A biblical reference to the "bread from heaven," symbolizing spiritual nourishment or the ultimate alchemical goal</note>, or heavenly food of eternal bliss, sighing continually after the enjoying of such*

<meta>catchword: riches</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author Basilius Valentinus reflects on his mortality, stating that his earthly work is ending as he prepares to transition from his physical laboratory to a spiritual, heavenly existence.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, alchemy, laboratory, Last Testament, spirituality, mortality, Manna</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory reflections on his spiritual motivations and his decision to publish his alchemical secrets.</meta>

# PREFACE

*riches*, which have a *fullness* of *everlasting joys*; I thought it my duty in *conscience* to be reconciled in a *Christian way* to my fellow members <note>meaning: fellow Christians or members of the human race</note> who are alive at present, and with those who are to *succeed* in *future ages*. I do this so that my *soul* may be at *rest*, patiently *waiting* for the *Lord's call*, and to become an *inhabitant* of that *heavenly Paradise*, and to be *matriculated* <note>original: "matriculated"; meaning: formally enrolled or registered</note> into the *book* of *life*, and *stand* in *readiness day and night* to *look for my Lord's coming*. 

At the *consideration* of this, I call to *mind* my *writings*, which I *set down* on *paper* just as other *ancient philosophers* have *done* before me. I am *publishing all such mysteries* of *nature* so that *artists* <note>original: "Artists"; in this context, practitioners of alchemy or the "Hermetic art"</note> and those who bear an *affection* and *love* for *such mystical truths* may be *benefited*. I *lovingly* and *readily leave* these truths to them, as *much* as the *highest* <term>Spagyric</term> <note>Spagyric: a term coined by Paracelsus referring to the production of herbal medicines or alchemical preparations through the process of separation and recombination (from the Greek words for "to draw out" and "to bind")</note> and *heavenly Physician* has *granted* and *revealed unto me*. 

*My conscience further* has *pressed me*, in the *pursuit* of a *Christian love* and the *performance* of my *promise dictated* by *nature*, to make a *larger account*. This is *because* it is *fitting* and *necessary* to *set forth* the *manuals* which *belong to this work*. These *ought* to be *described in detail* to *fully inform* the *judgment* of *men who are devoted to this study*. *Namely*, I will show *how nature's bolts*—which she has *pushed forward* in *various ways* to *strongly lock* her *secrets*—are to be *pushed back*. In this way, the *doors* of *worldly treasures* might be *unlocked*, the *knowledge* of *transcendent mysteries* may be *attained*, and—upon *serious, continued prayers* *unto* the *Creator*—*men's judgment* and *understanding* might be the *more* *encouraged* <note>original: "egg'd on"</note>.

<summary>The author explains that his Christian conscience and desire for eternal life have prompted him to publish his alchemical secrets for the benefit of future generations. He intends to provide detailed instructions on how to "unlock" the hidden mysteries of nature through both practical work and prayer.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Christian duty, Spagyric, Book of Life, secrets of nature, spiritual preparation, philosophical manuals</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory reflections on his spiritual motivations and his decision to publish his alchemical secrets.</meta>

# PREFACE

I am not compelled by either force or poverty <note>original: "indigencie"</note>, nor by vanity <note>original: "vain-glorie"</note> or selfish motives, to record these words and leave them to posterity. Only a simple consideration of the frailty and the misery of this world—where the "children of darkness" are almost completely lost in their groping ways—has caused me to do so. I cannot express how much my mind is troubled when I think of the folly of this frail world and consider the <term>Cimmerian darkness</term> <note>Cimmerian darkness: a mythological reference to the Cimmerians, who supposedly lived in a land of perpetual mist and gloom; here it signifies total spiritual or intellectual ignorance.</note> of its inhabitants. They think themselves to be of deep understanding when they have heard some foolishness <note>original: "fopperies"</note> discussed by their *university chairmen* <note>meaning: university professors or academic authorities</note>, supposing themselves to be greatly enlightened in their understanding because of it. 

Where is that high and precious *Medicine* of the ancient *philosophers* who lived before any *university* was ever mentioned or heard of? They received their *revelations* through earnest, pious prayer to *God*, joining to that their daily investigations into natural things and putting their own hands to the work. Where, I ask, has their painstaking effort <note>original: "painfulnesse"</note> and industry fled? Surely to no other place than to the horrid, impure, and filthy *apothecaries'* shops. It has been deeply cast down and buried in the earth, sunk so deep that neither the ringing or sounding of bells nor the beating of *mortars* is able to rouse it. Thus, their praise and the glory of the Almighty is at a standstill! And who produces any good from it? 

I am convinced of this: if my writings are brought to light after my death, and if a wise posterity—their *apostles* and *disciples*—read, meditate upon, and understand them well, and if they seek and delve <note>original: "dive"</note> into them with a *Christian* intention...

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author explains that his motivation for writing is not fame or money, but a desire to help those lost in ignorance and to revive the lost alchemical wisdom of the ancient philosophers. He criticizes modern universities and apothecaries for losing the spiritual and practical essence of true natural science.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Cimmerian darkness, university professors, ancient philosophers, medicine, apothecaries, Christian intention, natural philosophy</keywords>

*talked about and to live with a high reputation; they are filled*

*drowned most lamentably! Woe, woe to you, children*
*of Satan <note>original: Sathan</note>! Here I do not intend to be long-winded*
*in my words, nor to bring in any such matters which are*
*irrelevant <note>original: heterogeneal</note> to my purpose. At the closing of my book,*
*I will be more extensive in my expressions, as far as the*
*Heavenly Prince shall enable me to do. There, as*
*a final conclusion <note>original: Corollarie</note> to all my writings, I shall add*
*certain things with this condition and plea: that all those*
*who intend to be genuine and ethical <note>original: conscionable</note> in their med-*
*icines <note>original: Medicaments</note> may help and cure their suffering fellow Chri-*
*stians, and search and inquire into such things which*
*God has ordained for such purposes and implanted*
*them into nature.*

<summary>The author denounces fraudulent, prideful physicians who seek only money and reputation, contrasting them with godly practitioners. He promises to provide a concluding guide to the natural medicines God provided for healing.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, introducing his "Last Testament" and emphasizing that this specific work is written in plain language rather than the obscure parables he used in his previous alchemical or philosophical works.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<sig>A 2</sig>

*This current book of mine deserves to be called "A Light unto Darkness" because other matters, which I have discussed through parables in my former writings—a style I used because it is appropriate for Philosophers <note>original: Philosophers; in this context, the term refers to alchemists or natural philosophers seeking the secrets of nature</note>—are declared in this, my final instruction. In this work, I speak in plain and clear words, describing and naming the matter <note>original: "the matter"; likely referring to the "Prima Materia" or the fundamental substance used in the alchemical Great Work</note> openly. I show the preparations from the beginning to the middle and the end, demonstrating and setting before the eyes of men the matter in general and in detail, confirming and justifying its truth, and distinguishing between a solid foundation and a false one <note>original: "betwixt the ground and no ground"</note> in such plain terms that even children may understand and feel it with their hands.*

*Because this book provides a different kind of knowledge than my other writings—in which I have not written so obscurely, nor used such subtleties as the ancients did who lived before me and ended their days happily—it also requires a special place to be stored, kept secret from the perverseness of men in the world. I do not want it to be buried with me to be prey and food for worms; instead, it shall be left above ground and kept secret from wicked men. My purpose is that it shall be placed in a secret location where no one shall come near it except the person for whom God has ordained it. Other writings of mine shall see the public light sooner.*

But know this, whoever you are into whose hands this my Last Testament comes, which contains the revelation of heavenly and earthly mysteries, it will hap-

<summary>The author introduces this book as his "Last Testament," a plain-language guide to alchemical mysteries intended to be kept secret from the wicked and reserved for those ordained by God. He contrasts its clarity with the obscure parables found in his earlier works and those of ancient philosophers.</summary>

<keywords>Last Testament, alchemy, Prima Materia, secrecy, parables, light unto darkness, divine ordination, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of why he has hidden his "Last Testament" in a secret place, expressing his desire that his alchemical knowledge should benefit a worthy successor rather than be lost to time.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*...happen to you by a divine providence. To whose custody, following the devotion I then performed, I committed it, depositing it in that secret place together with other things. I was not forced by any necessity or hardship to leave it there, but did so only for the person whom God’s goodness shall appoint to find it. For it is not good for me to allow **God’s** creatures and mysteries—which are already too obscure and have stepped from the light into darkness because of the malicious perverseness of the wicked world—to die with me, as envious men are often inclined to do with the gifts they have been entrusted with. Instead, I will leave a glimpse of truth and of the clear heaven, thinking it befitting to clear my conscience by putting my talent <note>original: "talent"; a reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14–30), meaning a God-given gift or skill that should be used and increased rather than hidden away</note> to use. Let the will of the Lord be done through the person He deems to be worthy of it, into whose care and diligence I commit it from now on and forever. For I, a **monk** <note>original: "Cloyster-man"</note> and an unworthy **servant** of the divine, infinite **Trinity**, confess and acknowledge that I would never have come so far in my knowledge of these endless mysteries—in the **analogy** <note>original: "Ana-logie"; here referring to the correspondence between physical substances and spiritual or celestial truths</note> of natural things, and in the improvement and changing of their qualities to provide a sure and strong foundation for this profound knowledge of the true **Medicine** <note>original: "Medicine"; in alchemical texts, this refers to the "Universal Medicine" or the Philosopher's Stone, believed to cure all diseases and perfect base metals</note>, according to this Ordinance (through which I am ready to do good to everyone who desires my help)—if not for God's grace. As I have done until now, my desire is that God would enable me to do the same until my last breath. I say I should never have attained it if, by God’s special goodness, grace, and mercy, several books had not come into my hands, written by ancient **masters**,*

<summary>The author explains that he has hidden his secret knowledge in a specific location to be found by someone chosen by God, viewing the sharing of his alchemical "talent" as a religious duty. He credits his deep understanding of natural transformations and the "true Medicine" to both divine mercy and the study of ancient texts.</summary>
<keywords>divine providence, alchemy, Cloister-man, Trinity, Universal Medicine, ancient masters, Parable of the Talents</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface, wherein he explains his motivation for leaving his writings behind. # PREFACE.

*...who departed this life a long time before me, causing great joy to me and stirring up within me a return of hearty thanks to God, who was so graciously pleased in His providence to bestow them on me in the monastery <note>original: "Cloyster"</note> before any of my fellows.*

*I speak this without any vainglory; I have done as much good with them for my fellow Christians as was ever possible for me to do, for which, next to God, I returned thanks even to the end of my days.*

*Now, since I can be a steward <note>original: "Steward"; meaning a caretaker of God's gifts and knowledge</note> no longer, I have done according to what my abilities would allow. Let others also be industrious and not lacking in their stewardship. I return mine into the faithful hands of God Almighty and deliver up to His divine power and glory—instead of the keys to the house—all the allotted mysteries set down here in my writing. I leave them to His disposal, to bestow them after my death upon whomever His divine will shall choose, so that they may be used for the knowledge of His praiseworthy name and for the benefit and help of those who lack necessities and health; and for the avoiding and shunning of all worldly pomp, pride, wantonness, luxury, rashness, pleasure, covetousness, and spitefulness or contempt of others.*

*O Lord God Almighty, merciful and gracious Father of Your only begotten Son Jesus Christ, who alone are the Lord of Sabaoth <note>original: "Sabaoth"; a Hebrew title for God meaning "Lord of Hosts"</note>, the principle of all things that are made by Your Word, and the infinite end of all—*

# PREFACE.

...creatures above and below; I, a poor miserable man and earthworm, return thanks with my babbling tongue from the innermost center of my heart to You, who have been pleased to enlighten me with the great light of Your heavenly and earthly wisdom, and the greatest mysteries of the created secrets and treasures of this world, together with Your divine saving Word, by which I learn to know Your almighty power and wonders. To You belongs eternal praise, honor, and glory, from eternity to eternity, because You have bestowed on me health and livelihood, strength, and ability to be helpful to my fellow Christians in their necessities and inflicted infirmities with those mystical healing medicines, together with such spiritual comforts to raise their drooping spirits. Lord, to You alone belongs power, might, and glory; to You is the praise, honor, and gratefulness for all the mercies and graces You have bestowed on me, and have preserved me in them until this, my great age and extreme weakness. O! You God of all graces and Father of all comforts, be not angry with me that I deliver up to You, my eternal Creator, the keys of my stewardship, wrapped up in this parchment <note>original: "Pergamene"</note>, according to the duty my calling and conscience demand. With these You permitted me to manage the household for most of my time, until now that You have called and foreseen me to be Your servant and steward, and have graciously provided that I should enjoy the noble, sweet fruits which were gathered in Your almonry <note>original: "Almonarie"; a place where alms are distributed</note> until my very end, which now, O Lord, lies in Your power. I beseech You...

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's warning against greed and the improper use of the book, urging the reader to use these medical secrets for the benefit of their neighbor rather than personal gain.</meta>

# PREFACE.

and grant the same by other means and ways without you. Therefore look to it, do not tempt the Lord your God, for he will not be mocked. Be silent and reserved; meditate on God’s punishment, which no man is able to outrun; keep a good intention; do not let your greedy mind run on how you may get an abundance of gold and silver riches and vanity. But before all things which are written herein, let this be your chief aim: how you may appear helpful in word and deed to promote the health of your Christian neighbor. Then you will have given and brought an offering of thanks, and God will bestow more upon you, and such revelations will come to you, more than you would have ever believed.

Therefore, instead of a perfect <term>physic</term> <note>medical</note> book, I have annexed at the end those precious medicines with which, even to this hour, I have successfully cured many difficult symptoms and diseases held by others to be incurable. These I recommend to you, along with the rest of the things contained in these writings, to your conscience: be wary and do not abuse any of them, as much as your life and soul are near and dear to you.

If you receive this faithful admonition and warning, which I give to you here and there at the beginning, middle, and end, and in other places also, and do accordingly, then you cannot be too thankful for these things which, through God’s permission, shall be revealed <note>original: "intimated"</note> and made known to you from this book.

## PREFACE.

*I divide this book of mine into five parts, each of which is subdivided into certain chapters and parts. As for the style used here, it should not be expected to be anything other than what is appropriate for a miner, according to the condition of mine works, waiving all rhetoric and poetical manners of eloquent expressions.*

### I.

*In the first part of my intended work, I will chiefly describe the nature and properties of mine-works, in which minerals and metals grow, concerning their first sperm <note>seed or primal essence</note>, nativity, quality, and property, as well as their exhalation and inhalation.*

### II.

*The second part shall be a kind of recapitulation of the first part, which shall also contain an account of the condition and occurrence of mines, ores, stones, passages, and clefts, along with their associated liquids, powers, and operations, such as gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, mercury, and the rest of the minerals.*

### III.

*In the third part is declared, in clear and literal expressions without any defect or obscurity, the Universal of this whole world, and how all philosophers before me, with me, and after me, have made that most*

# THE FIRST BOOK,

## Wherein are shown MINE-WORKS, from where they have their Existence, Natures, and Properties: divided into the following Chapters.

---

### CHAP. I.

#### *Of the Ethereal liquor of Metals, or of the metal Ferch.*

<image-desc>An elaborate woodcut headpiece spanning the width of the text block, containing symmetrical foliate scrolls, flowers, and a central cherubic figure or mask.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A square woodcut decorative initial 'G' with a foliate border background.</image-desc>
God has created things under ground as well as the things above ground: By the things under ground, I understand Metals and Minerals, in which there is implanted also a fertility to their seed, without which the seed could neither grow nor increase. Seed which is barren does not have that fertility: by which it is inferred, that there is some distinctness between

->VALENTINUS BASILIUS.<-

## CHAP. II.

### *Of the seed of Metals.*

All those authors who have written about the metallic seed agree in this: they say sulphur is the masculine seed of metals, and mercury is the feminine seed; which saying must be taken in its genuine sense, for common sulphur and common mercury are not meant by it. For the visible mercury of metals is itself a body made out of bodies, and so it cannot be a seed; and being cold, its coldness *in itself* cannot be a seed. And the sulphur of metals being a food, how can it be a seed? Indeed, a seed consumes sulphur; how can one seed destroy the other? If so, what body would it produce? It is therefore an error if that should be taken in the common sense. If the mercury of bodies is in a work and has taken food, then all the six <note>The original "sex" is a period spelling for the number "six."</note> mercuries protrude one body; as one of the six is in its predominance, so the body arises.

Seeing there are seven of these mercuries, it happens that when the seed of *Mars* and *Venus* has the predominance, they produce a masculine body of *Sol* <note>Gold</note>, but if the seed of *Saturn* and *Jupiter* predominates, then a feminine body is produced, which is called *Luna* <note>Silver</note>; mercury is an assistant on both sides.

The same happens to other bodies: but these are always and in each and every work together; for they are indivisible, as is also appropriate, for what kind of body could be produced otherwise? For Nature has perfect bodies; though in themselves they must be dissolved again, yet they are perfect for, and in, their time. For what kind of seed would that be if it were defective in any of its branches?

Therefore every body has its perfect seed; hence transmutation has its ground in the ascension and descension to

<meta>catchword: of fall</meta>

<summary>This chapter clarifies that the "mercury" and "sulphur" described as the seeds of metals are not the common substances, but alchemical principles. It explains that the specific combination and predominance of these metallic seeds determine whether a metal becomes masculine (gold) or feminine (silver).</summary>
<keywords>metallic seed, sulphur, mercury, Mars, Venus, Sol, Saturn, Jupiter, Luna, alchemy, transmutation, masculine and feminine seeds</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the metallic seed from Chapter II, explaining how metals are homogeneous in nature and detailing the seven components that form their bodies.</meta>

of metals, which otherwise could not be if they were not homogeneous in their seed. For if any man says that silver is not gold, fools believe that also, because they have no fundamental knowledge of the seed—how it is to pass from one body into another—or else it lacks its fertility; nor can it naturally exist without a body in which it rests. There belong seven distinct parts to an unformed body of the metals to bring it by nourishment into a form, *namely:* 1. An earth. 2. A stone. 3. An earth-ash. 4. Earthly streams. 5. Glass or subterranean metal. 6. The subterranean tincture. 7. The subterranean *fuligo* <note>Soot or fume</note> or seed (fume). All these are the materials of the body; and as earth is man's matter, out of which God made him and to which he must return again, so all other bodies also at last return to earth. That miner who is judicious and knowledgeable in this, I judge him to deserve the name of a Miner. For there are but few of them who are rightly informed in this, or have any fundamental knowledge of it, though they are daily employed in it. Though some might say they could not help but be knowledgeable in their profession, yet it is not really so; if it were, what right use could they make of them? They put wrong names upon them and are ignorant of their utility, and this is the reason why they many times discard them as waste upon the heaps, where after some time they turn to goodness; and the longer they lie there, the better they become. This instruction deserves no hatred, but rather a grateful acknowledgment.

Why should philosophers be believed to know anything? But where is it written that men should seek and find except in their books? Where are learned artificial finings, infuliginations <note>The process of turning into soot or fume</note>, incinerations, and nutritions, if not from them? The seed of metals, as it is perfect, so is its *Ferch* <note>The life force or "ethereal liquor" mentioned previously</note>, or invisible life.

Where do those men stand who wish to work according to nature yet know none of these things, nor do they know where to get them? Yet they attack artists, exclaiming that they are false, as are all those who are employed in their ways; but we see and

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: hear</meta>

<summary>The author describes the seven material components of a metal's body and argues that metals are homogeneous in their seed. He criticizes ignorant miners who discard valuable materials and defends the expertise of philosophical artists against unlearned critics.</summary>
<keywords>metallic seed, homogeneous, seven parts of metals, fuligo, Ferch, mining, alchemy, philosophical knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the metallic seed, explaining the necessity of dissolution and the roles of the smelter and alchemist in extracting the seed, before transitioning to Chapter III regarding the nourishment of metals.</meta>

Hear how ignorance runs on. It is impossible to obtain a body without seed; that would be as much as to say a seed lacks fertility. Therefore, examine it exactly in its dissolution; the reduction of it will reveal its body. Work cheerfully.

But this is none of the smallest tasks, as some of the most ancient philosophers have said, who called it a double work. For thus they say: the metal must first pass through the smelter's hand, and afterward it must come into the hands of the alchemist, if the seed is to be known in the laboratory work. They mean or suggest by this saying that there is a twofold dissolution. The first is when the expert smelter brings the brittle body, by its natural conduction, into a state of malleability, by which its impurity is removed. Then comes the alchemist, who reduces the body into ashes, calxes, glasses, colors, and subterranean fumes, in which the seed of metals resides, and the *Ferch* <note>The vital spirit or life-force of the metal</note> or life is found fertile in the body. It is then reducible into a spiritual water or *prima materia* <note>First matter</note>, according to the quality and property of the metal, and is artificially divided into its natural principles according to the processes of the chemical art, of which more shall be spoken in another place when I shall treat of minerals.

---

## CHAP. III.

### *Of the Metallic Nutriment.*

Although it does not belong to this place to discuss how *mineralia-fossilia* <note>Minerals or fossils dug from the earth</note> are made underground, I will nonetheless give a hint of it: how nature makes them out of subterranean moist liquors and mine-growths, which afterward serve as food for metals. These are not such liquors as are boiled above ground; therefore, if you should add here, above ground, boiled ones to metals, undissolved in their corporeal

<summary>The author describes the "double work" of the smelter and alchemist required to extract a metal's fertile seed and introduces the concept of metallic nourishment derived from subterranean liquors.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, metallic seed, dissolution, Ferch, prima materia, smelting, mineralia-fossilia, metallic nourishment</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallic nutriment, explaining that while minerals are found in various geographic locations, they must be refined through preparation and sublimation to be useful in the alchemical art.</meta>

corporeal form, your work would be in vain; and where there are such *mineralia fossilia* <note>minerals dug from the earth</note> there are mine works also; if not right with it, yet they are not far off, as is seen in many mines. As in *Hungary* are dug the fairest and best sulfur-alums and mineral or mine vitriol. And about *Harcynia* <note>The Harz Mountains</note> are dug salt vitriol. About *Goslar*, *Mansfeld*, *Zellerfeld*, and at *Eisthland* in *Helvetia* <note>Switzerland</note> is dug mine salt, and at *Hall* there is a great store of it, where very curious sulfur is also found. But you must note that these minerals are not used so crudely, but are prepared first, which is a meticulous work to bring a mineral so high by subliming it into *flores* <note>flowers or powders produced by sublimation</note>, which are half-metallic—especially if made with metal. The metal being reduced into a mineral, from that the flowers are made; thus you see nature's readiness, and how she is reducible to her first water, sulfur, and salt. Many make these flowers without metal, which are not as good, however, as the former way. For an *oleum* <note>oil</note> made of vitriol or copper, which is distilled, is more effectual—yes, a thousand times more precious in its operation—than that which is made of common vitriol, which nature has not yet exalted. It is true that Hungarian vitriol, in its efficacy and virtue, is found wonderful and sufficient enough, because Nature has graduated it to a greater *siccitie* <note>dryness</note> and brought it to a ripeness more than others were, and it is more excellent than the rest. By this preparation, they can make use of the minerals and strengthen and increase their pleasure thereby. If anything is to be made fit for metals, then it must be done out of metals, with metals, and through or by metals, which is the real and only manual <note>practical method or handbook</note> by which the hardness of the mineral flowers may be hit; always take from them and add nothing to them. This is the Art, which requires great wonder and deep meditation. Thus you must learn to go to work, for these flowers are often found closely compacted, which miners very seldom know; especially in *Hungary* and *Wallachia*, they are as beautiful as any red glowing ore may be. They are of a crystalline transparent redness and are good gold and silver according to how they are tinged. This is a

<sig>B 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: rare</meta>

<summary>The author identifies various regions where minerals like vitriol and salt are mined, explaining that these must be prepared into metallic "flowers" through sublimation. He asserts that true alchemical transformation requires working with metals themselves, specifically praising the quality of Hungarian vitriol.</summary>
<keywords>mineralia fossilia, vitriol, sulfur, sublimation, flores, Hungary, Harcynia, metallurgy, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mineral preparation, moving from the creation of "flowers" (sublimated powders) to the necessity of removing impurities to ensure the quality of the resulting metal and the safety of the laboratory worker.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

->VALENTINUS BASILIUS<-

<warning>Bleed-through from the reverse page and some staining throughout, especially on the left edge.</warning>

...rare knowledge, an art worthy of the best consideration, which is to make glass of a hardness; from this it is that the subterranean glasses make up the metal, and thereby they come to their form.

The preparation of these flowers has great utility in medicinal ways, if their waste and their odors are removed from them. This waste is the dregs of minerals and is worthless for metals, stirring up evil sediments which bring damage to metals. A twofold evil comes from the mistaking of minerals: for decocted ones are a dangerous poison and corrosive to metals, as we see above ground when *aquafortis* <note>nitric acid</note> is made from them, which corrodes, tears, divides, and parts metals; and the others, which are fair to look upon, stick to metals and are their worst poison. For as soon as these approach, they kindle and cause dangerous sediments, and all avails them nothing, even though they have and keep their form. Just as an infected man still has the form and face of a man, even though he is infected and infects others also; and in case it turns all to one metal, yet it is but an empty one with nothing in it. 

This is a very necessary observation for miners and laboratory workers; for if they do not regard it, they obstruct not only their work but endanger themselves also. This is because the metal is not only turned into a volatile substance if any dregs or waste are added to it, but that which remains also becomes unmalleable and suffers continual loss as long as it is under the hammer. Those who work them have cause to look to it; if they attack them with any fire, their reward surely is some mine disease, as it is experimentally known how their poison sticks and hangs on the top of the furnace and in their chests, turning to arsenic and such poisonous fumes and seeds, and doing hurt in every way, as woeful experience evidences.

<meta>catchword: C H A P.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that minerals must be purified of their dregs and odors to be useful, as impurities can corrode metals and cause "mine disease" in workers. He warns that improper treatment of minerals produces dangerous substances like arsenic and corrosive acids.</summary>

## C H A P. I V.

### *Of the metallic shop*, Officina metallorum.

**A**ll natural works have their special, suitable places in which they work; wherever there is any such place or shop in which some glorious and precious thing is made—and sometimes, though the instrument be very horrid and monstrous and its matter unknown—yet they exist in that laboratory <note>officine</note>.

First, concerning the glory and praise of this laboratory, it is likened to a church, in which the seed and the Earth are married to the body; therein they eat, rest, and work. Thither they carry all fair and pleasant materials underground wherewith they are clothed, and they have another kind of fire, water, air, and earth. For the things that are accomplished and perfected therein can hardly be parted again, not even with the help of the lower <note>nether</note> air. If it must be parted asunder, then see and make trial of it on the mercury of metals.

Again, the things made so hard and fixed cannot be parted, as may be seen by gold: how firm and fixed is it in the fire? The cause of this is the subterranean heat and cold which it imparts to metals and makes them firm thereby; for it is a stony firmament of the earth. In giving to metals their stony power, it grows hollow and spongy, full of pores, which at last are filled up with metals, even as bees fill their hives with honey; and in the end, it parts and is carried away in the shavings or dust <note>flick or *Scobes*</note>. For the Earth-stone is not consumed underground because it is a sediment that does not allow anything to come in or out. Hence is that difference between the Earth-stone and the terrestrial-firmamental stone, which is one of the mineral works. Let no man deny that a stone should have both heat

This is its path and instrument: the heat and cold of the subterranean fire-stone.

The modern chemists, who are ignorant, not knowing Nature aright and failing to take notice of her ways, use strange instruments; they make, or cause to be made, all manner of vessels according to whatever fancy they have. But of Nature's ways they know little; she regards not the variety of forms, and instead of these, she takes a fit and lasting instrument which holds up in the work, and every form follows or accompanies the seed’s precedence. The folly and ignorance of workmen is aggravated by the fact that they despise the knowledge of minerals.

I should mention the instrument she uses for this here, but I will waive it at this time and will do so in another place, where you may seek it out and take notice of it. Those who think themselves to be the wisest say that it is a vanity to mathematically observe the stars above and to order any work according to seasonable days and hours; this is said by some, but it is not well-grounded. But this is most certain: if you work according to the common course, otherwise than we do, following only your own fancies, then your labor is in vain. There is a difference to be made between the upper stars and the metallic stars, which shine and have their influence upon the bodies. Touching the stars above, they have a singular influence in their light and motion; and the stars below have their influence also upon their metals. Thus, each heaven has its peculiar course and instrument, where the stars' situation may be understood. An *opacum corpus stellarum compact* astral body differs in its condition from a *corpus lucidum*. If you intend to learn something here, then you must be industrious and grudge no pains; it would require

It would be a huge volume if I were to describe in detail the whole circumference of subterranean mine works. It would not be enough to only name the things, but I must also demonstrate that everything I attribute to them is true. I say it would require a great deal of writing to dispose the minds of mistaken men toward a belief. What should I say of such materials, upon which I could not impose fitting names even if I knew them? For who is that man who has completed his learning in our school? Here I must speak as is proper to this place: no volume in this world can be written in which every particular *manual* [practice] could be set down, as Laborators might sometimes ask. Therefore, an Artist, having been given some hints of things, must endeavor to order his work and manage it judiciously; he must put his hands to the work and gain knowledge by his own practice. I direct such men in their work to Nature's process underground; let them search there and take an honest miner along to show them her instruments and materials (for bragging, lying, and ignorance avail nothing here). Everyone wishes to obtain riches, but the means for obtaining them are not always appropriate. If I were the best limner and could set forth in colors the form of any instrument, then men would understand it; it would help in this case, for they would see it and feel it with their hands and undertake the work if everything were set down. I know what and how much ought to be put in a book; I have put in fitting things and did so faithfully.

### *Of Egression and Ingression of Metals.*

The work of metals demonstrates a perpetual going in and coming out; for by this, the Egression is understood not only as the Egression of the whole work, that in

In some places, a whole metallic tract decays when it lacks the food to be nourished further and has devoured all its bodies, but there is also a partial egression <note>outward movement</note>; for one still seeks the other and follows at its heels. This we see by the mercury of metals: when poured out, it is scattered into thousands of little quick corns <note>grains or particles</note>, and all of them return to their body. *Volatile and Lubricum* <note>slippery or fluid substances</note> are in the same condition. The <term>Ferch</term> <note>a seminal or vital metallic principle</note> also goes forth in small bits, and at last it joins into a body somewhere, just as bees meet together. It receives no more than it needs; the surplus swarms to another mine-workshop <note>Myne-officine</note>, which, by parting and distributing, provides many and various mine works, according to the disposition of the workshop and the nourishment, and according to how it is hampered in its work during the egression. The <term>Ferch</term> and the seed continue in their volatility; and if they had wings, that volatile substance is so thin that it can hardly be discerned, yet it is foliated like a heap of atoms. Thus subtly it flies away, and the <term>Ferch</term> must still have its feed, the feed its body, and the body its thin atoms. 

My meaning is not that its egression is from or out of the earth into the air, to fly about there and then return to the ground again; this is not so, nor can it be, because its natural work is not in the air, unless men bring it forth purposely—then it is of another condition. I do not speak of that egression here, because it is done by day; but this goes through the earth, which stands in the furnace, neither perceptible nor visible to us, and runs through clefts and passages. For the earth gives way to the ingression <note>inward movement</note> and egression, just as the water does to fish and the air to birds, as long as metals come to their stone-firmament. This stone-firmament differs from the earth-firmament; when it meets with that, it goes around, looking for another passage, like water that flows around a stone and not through it. Yet it stays in its own stone and receives strength from it, and there turns into a body. And as it goes in its egression from one metallic firmament or stone-firmament to another, whether it is eaten through or soaked, at whatever distance it may be, it attracts

...draws the <term>*Lubricum*</term> <note>a slippery or fluid metallic substance</note>, just as a bird draws its feet up to its body in its flight; for if they touch anywhere, then they lose some of the body, and the <term>*Lubricum*</term> in its ingression <note>inward movement</note> allows it to return to a strength of operation. For when both are joined, then the metal increases and attracts its food in a wonderful way and nourishes itself; and it is to be admired that in this ingression, when that <term>*Lubricum*</term> comes more and more to its <term>officine</term> <note>workshop</note>, how it increases and strengthens itself so long that at last the work is made firm in the <term>officine</term>. This strengthening cannot be understood to be anything other than what the metallic <term>*mercury*</term> does; for in the first place, it turns it there into a <term>*liquidum*</term> <note>liquid state</note>, where afterward it receives everything and does coagulate and congeal, according to whether the bodies are either *masculine* or *feminine*. At last, it is brought to a solid, fixed body of <term>*Sol*</term> <note>gold</note>. This ingression makes that subterranean place noble and fruitful, and it is remarkable that when it has an ascending ore in work, that air is very wholesome; and if the air above from melting is not infected with arsenic fumes, then it affords a healthy air to dwell in.

This is a <term>manuduction</term> <note>guidance or introduction</note> to the whole subsequent work, how it ought to be proceeded in, so that the ore may stand and not awaken, but turn to its stream and still abide in its body's company. It is reluctant to make an egression <note>outward movement</note> if it once made a true ingression and settled itself to the work; for it does not rest in its place, nor does it rest in its whole tract, but works continually. It is clearly seen what its fixing or flight <note>volatility</note> is, and where it attaches <note>OCR "fetteth"</note> to a fluid body or earth-salt, which it stirs and rolls so long—yes, it pants and moves in it so long—until it gets a liquid body, then turns it to an earthly body, and is still brought on to a further height and hardness. And that is the right coagulating, congealing, liquidating, and fixing of <term>*mercury*</term>; which, if done accordingly, then it affords something.

### C H A P. V I.

## Of the dissolution and reduction of metals.

It is apparent that natural heat is the cause of the fluidity of metals' dissolution, because the seed of metals in itself is very hot, and the fluid matter of metals is hot also, as being <term>oleaginous</term> <note>oily or fatty</note>, and its heat increases when it comes to its <term>officine</term> <note>workshop</note>, because that also being hot increases the heat the more. This is why it is hot in the work and has need of it, for at first it would bring no more into its body unless it were soluble and soft; it brings nothing into it unless it is passed through these three heats and fixed by them. Then examine it, and add another fluid thing to it which did not pass through the three heats: see whether the metal will receive it or not? 

Secondly, they must be dissolved so that they may be cleansed; the condition of liquid things is to produce to the outside things fitting the work it has in hand. This solution is distinct from other artificial dissolutions where the body is only melted, as by the Melter when he separates the <term>excrements</term> <note>waste or impurities</note> from it; for nature does not melt the earth as men do, but as corn grows above ground, so she leaves corn and husks together. There is a great difference between our melting and Nature's dissolving; if we could observe that distinction in our dissolutions and meltings, we should not suffer such great losses and damages as we do. 

I must mention *aurum potabile* <note>potable or drinkable gold</note>, and how men busy themselves about it; as many heads as there are, just as many ways they choose for the making of it. Some take that which is not yet separated from the metal, still containing the cinders of waste, or worse things. They take corrosive waters, <term>*acetum aquavite*</term> <note>vinegar of the water of life</note>, and the like: pray tell me, what does Nature take when she is about the dissolving of frozen water?

<summary>Basilius Valentinus explains that natural heat is the primary cause of metallic dissolution and purification, distinguishing Nature's subtle process from the crude melting performed by men. He critiques contemporary alchemists for using corrosive substances to create "potable gold," arguing they should follow Nature's simpler methods.</summary>
<keywords>natural heat, dissolution of metals, metallic seed, officine, aurum potabile, purification, alchemy, acetum aquavite</keywords>

water? She takes none of these things, only making use of a heat. You must do the same if you will take a metallic body which Nature has perfected and which has come to us through melting and refining. If you will dissolve and reduce it to its first matter, then rouse the <term>Ferch</term> <note>life-force or internal spark</note>; thus you may make any metallic body potable. Once it is made pure and superfine, its <term>excrements</term> <note>impurities or waste</note> are gone, produced not with the addition of corrosive things—the fluxing of such matters rather makes the metals harder. If a body is to be fixed, we fix it from the outside, which Nature does not do; for she fixes the seed, then the flour sets and turns to such a fixation that dissolving above ground cannot master it. A water which congeals has at first a little crust, continuing in it until it is quite congealed; but here it congeals from the inside to the outside. Hence you may guess at that glorious foundation of projection upon the mercury of the body, making a natural *stratum super stratum* <note>layer upon layer</note>; thus are the metals joined, according to which the artificial work is ordered. We are given a hint of how the mercury of metals is clipped and alloyed, and its *lubricrum* <note>slipperiness or fluidity</note> is caught. Do not conceive of this fixation as being like when iron is hardened into steel and then reduced to a softness such as tin has; this is called only a close hardness, which keeps the body in a state of malleability and keeps it so closely bonded together that fire above ground cannot hurt it. All hardness created above ground may be softened in fire, but not the other, because it withstands all fiery trials. Therefore, just as the hardness made above ground hardens bodies in the water, so on the other hand, the water which is in metallic bodies must be taken out, and then it congeals. The subterranean air hardens the earth; the earth remains earth and does not turn to stone, and that same air keeps the water from running together or congealing, preventing it from turning into pearls and precious stones, which may be made from that water. To extract the internal fire from metals, though it requires the highest skill, is nevertheless feasible and found in its place where I write of such things in a more ample manner. ## CHAP. VII.

->*Of the ascension and descension of metals.*<-

This new kind or manner of speaking and writing of metals is caused by experience, for the first perpetual ingression of the <term>Ferch</term> <note>life-force or internal spark</note> increases and strengthens at first in the <term>officina</term> <note>workshop or laboratory</note> and <term>Matrix</term> <note>womb or source</note>, the Mercury of bodies, bringing it to its perfect and full strength. Being made wholly effectual and potent, then it begins by degrees to clothe itself with a body; at first, it attracts and receives the meanest <note>lowest or most base</note>, which it puts off again in the first place, which is done more easily; for no body among them all is sooner put off. For the body of Saturn <note>lead</note> is so thin, that it appears to the eye just as a fair body does through lawn or tiffany <note>fine, thin, semi-transparent fabrics</note>; its spirituality appears through its body. Its spiritual body is the metal of Mercury, or as I should rather call it, its proper, near, and special body; this work gives a <term>manuduction</term> <note>guidance or leading by the hand</note> to many other fair works. For it makes a garment for Saturn out of the subtlest earth; after he rises higher, he puts a harder and better garment on him, which is not so easily put off as that of Saturn, or at least not with so small a work, which is caused by the work of the Mercury of bodies. For the Mercury of bodies, by reason of its fluidity, is the hottest, as he makes it appear in Saturn's ascension, putting a cinereal <note>ash-colored</note> body upon—

of earth; this is why Saturn is so full of cinders, inclining toward a brittleness like ashes. It begins to sound like a metal, although it is not very firm; however, it is at the next stage for incorporation. Its sound is more dull; it is further from iron and nearer to mercury because of heat. Observe now that as this metal ascends, it lies near the ashes, cleansed by the Saturnal water. But above ground, it does not turn into glass from the ashes, nor from salt or earth-water, nor Saturnal water, nor from sand or stone. 

But what is that pure subterranean Earth-glass, which sounds when struck but does not break? It is a substance that Nature thrusts together into a heap; if you touch it, it rings, and it is very clear, of great compactness, and very firm. In this work, it mingles with ashes and salt water and turns into a glass of earth, or into a dark, glassy, firm iron. 

Indeed, tell me: if a metal or Earth-color—yes, a good, solid metal—is dissolved into a color and turned into glass, does it not look copper-colored? Yes, truly. Therefore, iron may soon be turned into another thing, which happens naturally; in *Hungaria* <note>Hungary</note>, such metallic iron color is reduced into a <term>Lixivium</term> <note>a lye or alkaline solution</note> and turned into very good copper. However, it retains its glassiness, even though the color has dried it somewhat through the mercury of bodies. It still retains its liquidity and is further ennobled into malleability and fixation. Therefore, take notice of this tincturing matter, which you find prepared by this body in this <term>officina</term> <note>workshop or laboratory</note>; it reduces iron to copper with a great deal of profit.

Put these colors aside and behold how the mercury of bodies has passed through many white bodies and still possesses a beautiful white <term>fuligo</term> <note>soot or volatile vapor</note>, which is very fixed. How finely it is clothed in it! It makes a beautiful and pure body of <term>Luna</term> <note>silver</note>, in which it clothes itself so strongly that it cannot be removed by burning, because it passed seven times through the greatest subterranean heat. This heat destroys corruptible bodies unless they are closely and compactly incorporated into the mercury of bodies. Nothing goes beyond

This is the reason why descending ores are more perfect than the ascending ones.

## CHAPTER VIII.

->Of respiring Metal, or of *Quick* ore.<-

Because through mining operations an obstruction is made upon Nature, which is the cause that several metals are obtained and distinctions made between them, so that damage and losses might be better avoided in the working of them. For as each received a particular name and property during the work, so in the digging of them, several manual methods were invented for finding them, and for this purpose use was made of the Rod <note>a dowsing or divining rod used to locate minerals</note>. To distinguish metals by colors is a skillful craft—such as Red gold glass, Mine green, or black ore; however, their working is not so exactly known by that way. So that I may lose nothing during their melting, I use this means: first, I must certainly know the property of the ore before it is beaten out, while it is yet underground in its "breathing." For ores and metals breathe only underground; though they breathe in some way above, that same breathing is very weak, not going far from the body. The rod also reacts only to the upper metals, which is the greatest advantage we have: for stir up

...causes metals to breathe into a flame; the fire-grates and pit-diggers cause only volatility and a dense compactness, from which a threefold damage follows. First, not only does much of the metal fly away [evaporate], but what remains also becomes volatile, and in its various meltings, something always goes wrong. Secondly, the remainder becomes unmalleable, which can hardly be corrected. Thirdly, elevated minerals are burnt into a dense mass; if this were not done, they would prove very advantageous in the later processing, and they would be especially useful in *Medicina* <note>medicine</note>, being naturally prepared for it. This is why many in their later workings labor in vain, using other improper minerals for that purpose. For instance, regarding that *mineral Vitriol* at *Goslar*—where neither silver nor lead grows in that mineral—when it is highly prepared, copper can be made from it without any other addition. That *Vitriol* also provides an oil that perfectly cures the Gout. If all these good qualities were to be burnt away with the silver, would it not be a great loss? Therefore, I take a metal that attracts breath, and when the "un-breathing" or adhalation <note>the act of breathing toward or upon something</note> is stronger, then it is a living metal because a life-like quality is in it. For breathing things are alive, and breathing is compared to life. Such a metal, like a breath, progresses like a child every ten years; even so, this metal grows until it reaches its perfect state and body. Afterward, it receives another name. Consequently, a great difference must be observed in how they are worked; they are as different as a living thing is from a dead one. This ought to be taken into consideration because observing this distinction ensures a neat and pure work. To this category belong all mixed ores, which are only separated during the refining process and not before (as is the usual custom). For example, in *Hungary*, gold-silver (that is, silver containing gold) is found everywhere; in its color and weight, it is pure, has lost nothing, and is still in its "working" quality. If it had not been interrupted and dug up prematurely, then that silver would have been turned...

For dead bodies constantly add more to it, making it heavier in the mercury; then the nourishment also joins in—which are the prepared minerals—providing their tincture like good food that produces good blood. This is how *<term>flores mineralium</term>* <note>flowers of minerals/sublimates</note> come to exist; if you cannot learn their origin in that way, there is no other or better way for it. For this is the true tincture, and not that moldy or coarse *<term>album</term>* <note>white</note> or *<term>rubeum</term>* <note>red</note> so called. Where such highly mixed ores or transparent veins break open, they may be praised as an *<term>Electrum</term>*, but they are improperly named so; for the colors glitter so purely within one another, as in a *<term>Chrysolithe</term>* or transparent *<term>Amber</term>*.

## C H A P. IX.

### *Of expiring metal, or of dead metal.*

Metals have their set time as well as all other creatures; they decay and die when their appointed time comes. For when Nature has brought the metallic body unto *Sol* <note>gold</note>, then because it lacks nourishment and is starving, it declines, receives a stronger exhalation, and the attractive breathing turns to an expiration, and aerial breathing brings it to the fire's breath. If the expiration grows stronger in a metal than its aspiration <note>inhalation or drawing in</note> is, then it descends by degrees and decays, and then it is called a dead ore or metal; for one external body or another continually departs from it, until at last, in one place or another, it makes a total departure with its breath, life, and seed. This breathing is known by the particular Rod of each; this also requires a particular place and work, because great gain is provided both by this and by the living one. Consider it well: a metal which descends from its perfection into another body is like when a man loses his lively color, and at last his body—that is its weight—and then the gold turns, not to a goldish silver, but to an *Electrum* <note>an alloy of gold and silver</note>; that is, to *Sol*, which has lost its tincture <note>characteristic color or essence</note>. This is a great piece of proof to discern such silver-gold from true silver; in its gravity it is found heavier than other silver, retaining the body and losing only its color or tincture.

It is a greater skill to restore a tincture to a dead and decayed color, and to make it stable. At separating, it keeps the quality of *Sol*. Red silver is in a similar condition when it has lost its color, making an incorporation and union with copper so that it quite dies in its body. To get this silver out of the copper and restore its peculiar color is a great skill, which smelters are ignorant of; it belongs

Other metals are of the same and similar condition. Is it not so that all iron in *Hungary* is brittle? What is the reason? It is because copper is in it. If that is extracted in that artificial manner as it ought to be, that iron proves so hard that no steel is comparable to it. Out of that iron are made Turkish swords (sabers) and mail armor, which no weapon or bullet can enter or break; these mail coats also are not very heavy.

Note: the *lubricum* <note>slippery or fluid quality</note> at the descending of mercury must have room; something may be obtained more easily from slippery things than from hard things. Slippery things always leave something behind; they do the same with their tinging bodies, shedding them continually so that, in the ascending of their *Volatile* <note>easily evaporated or refined essence</note>, they assume the body and elevate it.

Note: if you have any material in hand and in your Afterwork <note>subsequent refining process</note> you would know whether you must keep to the *Lubricum* or *Volatile* (these two must be your help), then your material must be prepared either the slippery or volatile way. If you want a body either to ascend or descend, take notice of the flux in the metal; slippery ones are more open than the hard ones. When tinctures begin to depart—which are a strengthener to the metal—then the material grows more fluid and comes closer together than in the living metal.

## CHAP. X.

### *Of pure or fine Metal.*

When a metal—whether in ascension or descension—is in its seven systems or constitution, then it rests or endures until it enters into another body. If you meet with such ore, it yields the purest metal that may be had.

Such metals, as may easily be understood, are pure, good, and malleable, losing nothing in any work whatsoever. Consider what kind of skill is required to get wind or air out of the water and to bring another mercury into that place. Bring yet another mercury of bodies into that water instead of the marine water; then you have a fair pearl. Take that same mercury of bodies and reduce it to an earth, which must be pure; instead of the air, you then have a pure jewel, as pure as may be had from that earth in its color—or you may add a color to it as you please; it is a feasible thing.

## CHAP. XI.

### Of Impure Metal.

A great abundance of metallic ores is found, but few of them are pure, and few grow or occur together; therefore, these must be separated and discussed separately. Let melters speak of the great effort and expense required for their cleaning due to their coarseness. Separating has been invented, by which some good parts of the ores remain while the rest fly away; their refining is useful, especially when ores or metals are in their ascension, though it is costly. But to find *Electrums* and bring them to a good state by separating differs from the former refining and requires a unique method of melting. Clever and subtle artists may pretend to extract silver from iron (I believe they may, if there is any in it), as they do in *Sweden*. *Osmond* <note>A type of high-quality iron ore</note> always contains silver, which is only driven off while the iron is calcined away, and thus they cheat people. Can they do the like with the iron that occurs in *Styria*? Not at all. Therefore, beware of such cheaters, and take notice that nature loves to follow her own orderly ways and keeps together two, and sometimes three, sorts of ores in their ascension and descension, by which she suggests a way for the subsequent work; but men in their imagination think of other methods, though to no purpose.

<summary>This page addresses the extraction and refinement of impure metallic ores, emphasizing that nature provides orderly pathways for the "after-work" of alchemy. The author warns against fraudulent artists who claim to extract silver from iron through deceptive means.</summary>

Look at all the mines which are in Europe, and you will find no ores but impure ones—that is, a mixture of them—for their nature makes them so, as far as I have ever been able to learn; if you can show me the contrary, I will agree with you. And this is the second argument: that metals are in a state of ascension and descension toward perfection. If each had its own unique work and instrument, then men would not need to take such pains in melting to separate them. For it is a difficult task to join weeds and stones because these are heterogeneous and are made of different materials; but others, joining easily together, require special effort to be separated. Therefore, observe the bodies carefully in two ways, which provides no small instruction. 

First, consider in what manner you separate the ashes from the fumes or "food"; this already provides a twofold separation of the metal—the earth from the tincture—and so forth. Secondly, take notice of the flux, driving the cold fire with the warm and the warm with the cold; then you will be able to separate the bodies from *mercuries*. Thus, you will have already separated the metals without loss or damage. Practice this precisely and be careful in observing their names, disregarding the miners' expressions and terms, for the names they give to ores are false. For those who build and dig along clefts and passages name their materials based on the types of minerals and are distinguished by them. But you must name them according to the sorts of the seven bodies and learn how to prepare them; this work is of much greater usefulness. 

Men go to great expense to obtain corrosive waters to pull these metals apart; they also do it by way of melting and casting. However, such waters add much poison to the work. It is a better way to do it with *lixiviums* <note>Alkaline solutions made from ashes</note> or sharp waters, which are not as poisonous; learn their preparation. There is another kind of impure ore, which I have mentioned here and there, which contains mine-slags; you may read of this in the chapter on cinders. But there is a difference between the cinder and the slag, for slag is more granular, yet that also turns at

These flakes are the cause of the cold fire, and cinders cause the warm fire, or the *uredines metallorum* <note>the burning or scorching of metals</note>: these cold flakes are hard to remove from the metal because they come from the cold flux fire of mercury’s altitude; for just as the cinder comes from bodies, so these flakes come from mercury. It is seen that when you wish to have flakes of other matters than those to which they usually belong, then the mercury of bodies is roused, which in the work is nothing else but a closure and stream; for if you can conveniently remove the flakes, then you may perform and accomplish something else with the fire of mercury. It is neither usual nor professional to deal much in cold fire: some miners call it *Mispuckel* <note>mispickel or arsenopyrite</note> or *Nodus æris* <note>knot of copper</note>—that is the Latin name they give it. It is true that it is very tightly knit together; it is difficult to dig it and to make its ore productive. Antimony is also hard to separate from other metals (except from *Sol* <note>gold</note>) without damage; however, it may be done skillfully to one's advantage, provided you are an expert in *Antimony's* qualities. For they belong together and are joined, as are tin and lead, or bismuth or *Magnesia* among or between iron and copper. This is a good direction and is sufficient for those who know what belongs to smelting.

### C H A P. X I I.

->Of perfect metal.<-

Who could tell what gold and silver were if they were not known in their perfection? For when they are perfect, it is apparent, as they have their color, their weight, their malleability, their flux, and hardness. Nature has produced this perfect metal compactly and purely; for such perfect, pure, and compact gold is found in *Hungary*, in white marble, which may be readily broken, as are silver and copper. Something is called "pure" because metals are not pure before they are perfect; and so there may be a perfect ore which is not pure. This defect is found in many of our metals, which reach their perfection as quickly as those in any other foreign parts, but they are sometimes defective in their perfect purity. This is to be noted regarding this metal: a body must first be perfect, or brought to its perfection, before it can be fixed; and it is of great importance to know rightly what fixedness means.

A body which has its due tincture, weight, and graduation may still have other obstructive impurities mixed in. Here the work begins, and nature starts to join these two: tincture and graduation bring the metal into a purification; this purification is fixation, for "pure" means the same as "fixed." And note that the foundation of the first is the body, which is a secret. Into that I must bring the tincture and graduation as well as I can, and take the tincture from <term>Sol</term> <note>gold</note>, which is a feasible thing. Then it is an <term>Electrum</term>, which is a water; for it remains in water. Then I take its weight for it and bring it into an <term>Oleum</term> <note>oil</note> or into a <term>Sulphur</term>. The body remains still, for in the ascension of <term>Mercury</term> lies the foundation of the body, like an *embryo*. To it comes weight, which makes it formal; then comes <term>Lubricum</term> <note>fluidity or slipperiness</note>; after that comes the <term>Volatile</term> with the tincture, and it perfects everything else it needs for its completion. Why does reason play the fool by despising the ways of nature and not observing her course? For behold how wonderfully she brings redness into copper, turning it into brass; but it is not fixed because that was not her intent. It is a mere color which all other ores easily embrace, but it is not fixed, and this color is easily driven away with wood and coal fires. Therefore, it is a matter of great importance to learn how to rightly know the bodies; for through dissolutions, the property of a pure metal is known—what its tincture, body, salt, and weight are—especially if the anatomy of all bodies is exactly considered in the chemical way, noting how curiously and properly they are anatomized. We call the immature

<summary>The author explains that a metal must reach perfection before it can be truly purified through fixation. He describes the alchemical process of combining a body with tincture, weight, and graduation to achieve a fixed state, using the example of brass as an unfixed coloration of copper.</summary>
<keywords>fixation, perfection, purity, tincture, Sol, Electrum, Mercury, copper, brass, chemical anatomy, graduation</keywords>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>his last Will and Testament.</header>

spirit, a spirit of mercury; the perfect tincture we call an *Anima* or *Sulphur*; the weight is called the salt or body. For the subsequent work confirms this: that this fixation does not only hold in the fire and in all corrosive waters (especially that of *Saturn* <note>lead</note>, which is a precious one, more so than other *aqua fortis*), but it is also better in its malleability without the <term>Quart</term> <note>quartation; a refining process where gold is alloyed with silver to be purified by acid</note>. It also holds in the cement, because it comes out of it. Therefore, it is surprising why men talk so strangely of it when they know nothing about it—where it comes from or what its cause is! But so it is: one listens to the tale of another and knows no more than what they have heard from hearsay. For they do not know what to say, nor how to fix what is wrong with the work if it is out of tune and disordered. If any fixed and lasting medicine is to be made, then that metal is best, just like a vegetable that has reached its maturity.

This process must be observed; otherwise, all labor is in vain. For how can you destroy the body of a thing that has no body? Much less can you have its tincture before it enters the body. A tincture may be obtained from it, but not all the colors of that tincture. Here, exactness must be used because it is the greatest skill to do so. One more thing I must speak of: those who aim merely with a great and deep longing for riches should view God's mysteries everywhere. If they compare the Scripture rightly, they will find instruction that a spirituality is represented in earthly things. If a metal is brought out of the earth and out of its *Officina* <note>workshop or laboratory</note>, like a man who is set into another life, it remains and lives without food; it is not dead, but alive. Although it does not act, yet it rests and may soon be awakened. Thus we also hope that in the other eternal life, where there is *Sabbathum* & *Sabbatho* <note>Latin: Sabbath after Sabbath; a state of eternal rest</note>, things will be done in a more glorious way. God makes use of us here for His praise, to make use of metals in that way; it will not be for our misuse and disgrace, but rather it will bring greater honor upon us—more than we ever put upon metals.
