## *C H A P. XIII.*

### *Of imperfect metal.*

The imperfect metal is the wildest among all the rest, because it contains all impurities with a confused mixture. Usually, there is set in a work of many pieces, which no man yet knows what they are, in which you find matters joined which are prepared and unprepared of many bodies. Now if you will reduce that matter and cleanse its metal, then the unprepared matter must first be washed away; and regard not the matter so much as the metal, which is yet tender and young in it. You must not calcine it, but another fire belongs to it. This perfection consists in nine separate pieces, which must be well considered; each must be handled as it ought, so that the metal shall not suffer any wrong or damage.
    First, consider well whether the ore be in ascension or descension; then you may help its color and whole body. For that metal which is in its ascension must be aided in its *folium* <note>leaf or foil</note>, but the metal which is in its descension must be helped with *Spolium* <note>waste or dross; the byproduct of refining</note>, otherwise it flies away because it is not perfected in its due place and *Officina* <note>workshop or laboratory</note>; and that metal is in a form accordingly. Thus make your proof, and be more encouraged in your subsequent work.
    Secondly, you must take notice of the nourishment or food which, as I may say, is not fully digested; promote the same in its concoction <note>the process of maturing or "cooking" through heat</note>, otherwise the excrements cannot well be removed, which would be the cause of unmalleability.
    Thirdly, you must take notice of the flux: that which is fluid must be fried; for if both fluxes should be opened, they would hardly be congealed again. For to dissolve

Fifthly, make a true distinction between the two imperfections; from these this metal has its name. One is of the body, the other is of the instrument. First, take the instrument in hand and endeavor to help the body in the preparation, and stop its flux or running into another, and you drive out the *Schwaden* <note>vapors or exhalations</note> or the cuticle of the seed; then the cuticle of the seed and the *Ferch* <note>life-force</note> or life go.

Sixthly, consider that fire which nature has, so that you do not encroach or entrench too far upon the bodies. Direct and order all your work upon the *uredines* <note>cleansing qualities or "soaps"</note> or soap; join it with your fire to the instrument of the body.

Seventhly, you ought to be well instructed about the tincture of the *Electrums*, so that you do not mistake *Sol* <note>gold</note> for *Luna* <note>silver</note>, because you are not experienced enough in the sleeping tincture or color, which you are to awaken in the descension, or which you are to strengthen and keep in the ascension. Painters have a term in their profession which they call elevating and shadowing; you must apply the same here to bodies when they are in their perfection. Therefore, anatomize the bodies.

Eighthly, we see *Limners* <note>painters or illustrators</note> mingle their colors with water and oil, and so carry them on. You must learn a proper preparation of the water to imbibe the color, which is a metalline water, by which you corrode without destroying the colors you intend to have. If you make use of any other water besides this, then all your labor

Lastly, take notice that the metal is regulated so that it looks for no more food. All will be but imperfect, therefore provide your help to it; you have nature's half-work for an advantage. With great pity, it has often been seen how such noble and precious instruments have been consumed in fire—which could have been used for several good purposes—because the workmen in their carelessness disregarded them. All other metals belong to this: for very seldom is a completely perfect metal found which does not lack one help or another. He who is acquainted with these will perform these works with greater advantage and more utility. For there must be an existing perfection if anything is to be purged, so that another pure or fixed thing may be introduced, whether it be the color or weight.

### CHAP. XIV.

->Of the Soap-metal, or *Metallum Uredinum* <note>cleansing metal</note>.<-

If our upper elements could not make a metal manifest and apparent to us, who would have undertaken to work any metal? The great heat and cold which is under the ground is the cause of the body of metal, according to the predominance of that heat and cold which is in the body. The deeper the heat or cold is in that body, the more beautiful is its tincture <note>color or essence</note>; this is a true saying. But what this heat or cold is above, and what is attributed to the planet above, men of understanding must know.

At first when the *Ferch* <note>vital life-force</note> goes forth, and travels through

This is the reason why the grains are so strangely fashioned: they are either round or oval, and so come to the metal accordingly, and are found. In this country, such springs are near *Rivers* at the foot of mountains, but in the *Althen* they are on the top of the soap, as it were, upside down. On these sticks the *Ferch* which passes by it, or the air which forces it from it. The whole business lies in the metallic waters as they are dissolved in their salts; thus they easily embrace the *Ferch*, or the metal of that *Ferch* and seed which it carries forth. And that goes for the best *Tin* which is on the soap, being of an iron-shot, which iron is of several different degrees; but here in this place it is not energetic, as being obstructed by two parties.

First, the water is an obstruction to it, for ore must give way to water; it is carried away. Such soap ore is seldom found by the *Uredines*, or in the soap.

Secondly, this *Scobes* <note>filings, powder, or dross</note> powder or dust is not in its right *officina*, because it is not in this subterranean air and element where the singular *officina* of metal is. In this condition are almost all the soap-metals in *Europe*. ### CHAPTER XV.

->*Of inhalation or inbreathing.*<-

It is a painstaking task to build secondary clefts and passages, for it seems believable that the breathings move in and out of them more than through the rest of the earth. However, it is not advisable to take such roundabout ways, because there is a nearer way to it—namely, to the metals—as if one wanted to go to the well and followed all the streams down, through and by all the springs, whereas there is a nearer way by taking the footpath. The same condition applies to the halations and breathings, because the fumes and vapors lie within them and are obstructive to the breathing rather than helpful. Therefore, do not look so much upon clefts and passages, but rather upon the halations or breathings, which keep their course throughout the earth, because the earth does not hinder its course as men might think. For we must know that the ores breathe only underground, and these breathings are orderly distinguished. This is a remarkable thing, that ores breathe from below where

It then introduces the concept of 'inbreathing' or 'halations,' arguing that miners should follow these natural vapors directly to find ore rather than following secondary passages.</summary>
<keywords>Ferch, Mercuries, officina, inhalation, inbreathing, halations, ores, descension, alchemy, mining</keywords>

where they are, and this is the difference between the halation, vapor, fume, or breath, which goes obliquely, crosswise, laterally, or far off. Therefore, here we must speak of that inhalation, when it goes in its own strength, what it shows and signifies. The fume is sent to the metal from the superior *solar rays*; from there, she <note>the metal or the earth personified</note> may have what she will, and what she has need of for her sustenance. That brings the nourishment and drives everything downward to the metal and the lower planets, but no farther than upon the earth; however, through the damp earth, *per cutem terræ* <note>through the skin of the earth</note>, there the ore or metal receives the inhalation which arises from the work and makes use of it. For it is manifest that the elements cannot reach deep into the ground. Seeing that the halations are invisible—their being cannot be seen in them—a man's breath can sooner be discerned and seen than the halation may; which may be known by a *Rotten* <note>likely a term for decomposed rock or a specific mineral indicator of "breathing" ore</note>: when a metal works, then it breathes, which breath it draws in again, and so forth. Then the Sun must come in to help, for if the *System* is at the inhalation, which must be known through art, there is the purest earth from which man was taken, as some ancient writers conceive; this earth contains many hidden virtues in the after-work of *Metals*.

This inhalation serves chiefly for that purpose which you heard: to draw, swallow, and take down the food into the body. For you must not think that she lets it lie in the body after she has brought it in, but it is a *Vis retentiva* <note>retentive power</note> to keep fast that which it drank in; and to that end, she prepares throughout that whole journey from the place where she receives or borrows from the Sun, which drives down all ponderous things, as we see by all such juices and saps which are driven downward. In this drawing down, it has this quality: whatever saps or juices it takes, the same sink down deeper and deeper, and it sublimes them more highly into its *flores* <note>flowers</note>. But I call these *flores* when it cannot sublime any further and brings it at last to a garment or body of a metal.

He who knows these flowers rightly has learned something of nature; and he who knows of what they are

It expels moisture and vapors; consequently, it causes those evil vapors, fumes, and breaths which poison the whole earth below and are the cause of many dangerous diseases, as is well known.

## CHAP. XVI.

->*Of exhalations or out-breathings.*<-

Since stirring and moving must be continuous in the metallic work as long as the metal attracts—and especially during the inward movement or absorption—the next inhalation of the living ore must be stronger. Therefore, Nature provides that because the halations <note>vapors or breaths</note> can go no further into the metal (since the metal is the end of the work underground), the exhalations must then take over and turn into the metal. The return of that metal's breathing from below into a true natural breathing of metals grows so strong that it kindles, yet without a light or flame; it glows without a flame or light and purges what needs to be purged in the work—not only in the work within the *officina*, but in the whole matter. This continues until she heaves and pushes out the pure atoms of the earth, which the Sun gloriously dissolves again into juices; for the atoms are the flowers of the terrene-salt <note>earthly salt</note>. She cannot endure them, nor are they useful in the earth, but they must be dissolved again in the upper air. However, this exhalation, though it

Even though it is always around the metal, it is apparent in its *Rottenness*, or *Rubedo* <note>redness, the final stage of the alchemical work</note>, what the ore does; then the breathing seizes on the *Rottenness* or *Rubedo* and kindles them. Then the metal is in its decay or decrease and is a dead metal, and most of the fire goes out, which kindles this *Red*, while the cold stays by the lower metal, which becomes predominant. From this, we see what *uredines* <note>blights or burning heats</note> are able to do when they are predominant in metals; then the cold gets the upper hand and degrades the ore, bringing it as low as lead. 

But if heat gets the upper hand, then the metal grows higher and higher and more lively. This is the difference between the heat of fires, which is called *Uredines*; the vehicle of that heat is the instrument or matter which provides the place for it. For *uredines* are the cause of something, but fire destroys it, and the *uredines* may exist without the fire and above the fire. The most ancient Philosophers call these *cœlestes* & *infernales vires* <note>celestial and infernal powers</note>; for it is apparent in gold that fire cannot prevail against it, except over its flux <note>its melting or flowing state</note>, and cannot consume it. Mercury cannot hurt it either; he only brings hardness to it, which is good, but to what purpose? For he is not turned into any silver. 

Both these breathings—the upper and lower, the inhalation and exhalation—come to the same thing. It is not only their quality to show and make known the metal and ore, but they also pass through the earth to keep them from turning into stones, to maintain them as porous one upon another, and to make them pure. For it purges the earth, just as the *solar* breathing above does the air; and as that same breathing above brings and causes wind, mist, rain, hoar frost, and snow, even so the subterranean breathing causes vapors, fumes, breaths, saps, minerals, soaps, etc., and brings forth gold out of their seed. He who understands these lower, upper, and other meteors may make all kinds of living After-works; for Art follows in the footsteps of Nature.

## CHAP. XVII.

->*Of coruscation, or of inhalation, or of breathing or glittering.*<-

Miners, seeing this breathing ascend by night like a flame, consider it to be only a breathing and nothing else; yet in itself it is not a true breathing, but only a coruscation. For it burns away the excrements—not entirely, but only such things that are worthless and poisonous—and purges the air of that same stench. For how should the husk of the seed get out if it were not carried forth, partly by this flame of fire and partly by the water? For this stench weakens the ore mightily, and especially it spoils its color; whereas it may easily be burned or washed away before it grows into such a stench, whether it be in fossils or liquids. This breathing or glittering strengthens the cinders; it does not touch them by way of heat, but by way of fire, and is not a boiling up, but a burning onto them.

Since this material which this coruscation seizes upon is often found in a place where no metals are, it is therefore very deceitful, even though it is commonly and for the most part from a metallic breath. For note: the metal never provides or casts any flame, nor does it consume in burning, but flies away and vanishes; you cannot know which way it has gone. Therefore these *exuviæ spermatis*, or husks of the seed, are a greater indication and clearer evidence that there is an ore at hand; because it makes many glorious things apparent, which the fire seizes upon, it must not be entirely melted away. Its departure is a sign that it does no good for the metals, nor can it stay with them, unless it sticks closely. Here you may observe again that fire does no good to metals, but rather a warmth which does not come from fire, but is a natural

<meta>catchword: tural</meta>

<summary>Basil Valentine describes "coruscation," a flame-like exhalation seen by miners that purges impurities and "stench" from metallic ores. He notes that while these flames indicate the presence of ore, they can be deceptive because the metal itself does not burn but rather vanishes when exposed to fire.</summary>
<keywords>coruscation, metallic breath, ore purification, exuviæ spermatis, alchemical fire, mining signs, Basil Valentine, inhalation</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>his last Will and Testament.</header>

natural heat exists in man without fire; for where are the coals which nature uses for fires, yet it is hot? Where is there any better blast to make things congeal? A cold without a frost is another matter, as it appears in pearls. I call it *Uredines*, like a warmth without a heat; indeed, this heat and frost provide a fuel for fire, yet it cannot endure it anywhere about it. Therefore, if you bring a metal into its heat and cold, then you have already a foundation for the afterwork; you have little learning or skill if you do not know this much.

# CHAP. XVIII.

## Of Folium & Spolium; *or of the Shimmer and Glimmer.*

The second or other thing which clearly shows and distinguishes metals is the color. The nature of color is such that metals are not easily discernible because of their darkness, in which they naturally exist; they are elevated and brought to a *Folium*, or are exalted through a *Folium*. Just as an obscure and opaque *folium* does the same in a *lucidum & translucidum corpus* <note>bright and transparent body</note>, so must a transparent *folium* do in a thick body. Since metals are an *opacum corpus* <note>opaque body</note>, shining like the moon with a borrowed light into the body, the *folium* gives it forth out of that body.

Such a *folium* or glitter may be made artificially, but nature shows that it may be obtained from a volatile substance. Although it is true that this volatile cannot be obtained except in its seed and body, yet it is soon present with the body; for he who does not know the nature of the *folium* can bring no ore or metal into its true tincture, either in the flux or congelation. The nature of this *Folium* is that

<meta>catchword: it</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the metallic "leaf" (folium), explaining its extreme thinness and its role in the composition of metallic bodies. It then introduces the next chapter on the "soots" or vapors of metals.</meta>

It is as thin as any leaf in the world can be; a metallic body is composed of these leaves.

Counterfeit chemists busy themselves very much with their *stratums* and *superstratums* <note>layered alchemical processes</note>, but here it is naturally thinner than beaten gold, and this is called *opaca perspicuita* <note>opaque clarity</note>, an in-brought leaf, not gilded or silvered. Here lies a curious skill for tinging with this leaf, if it is brought into the *Glimmer*; one piece of knowledge is the cause of another. As it is seen in a glowing and glittering splendor, there is no *exuviae spermatis* <note>hulls or skins of the seed</note> or hull of the seed at hand; nor may it be known in any *folium* <note>leaf</note> or *spolium* <note>shimmer</note> by its *speculums* <note>mirrors</note> or night lights, how to prepare that leaf and *spolium* which soon loosens and frees itself. This is the most important thing, for it departs with the most potent poison and arrives with it as well, and it must settle and rest.

## CHAP. XIX.

### *Of smoky vapors and ashes.*

Concerning the white soot of metals, which is a precious garment for silver if only brought into *mercuries*; it is wonderful to behold how the corruption of metals and of the primary bodies causes the highest and uppermost parts to become the lowermost. Where these soots are found, metal is surely at hand also; but a fairer and more natural soot is hardly seen than that which exists in *Styria*, where they make good steel from it. In *Wallachia*, it breaks out or is found near silver, and this is the surest thing in metallic work—both in the Natural work and in the Afterwork—which seems most incredible to people. And again, that which is most uncertain proves to be most certain; the reason for this is that they do not know or understand it any better. Miners have things hanging and lying in their clefts and passages from which great matters might be obtained and found, but

...require great toil and is a dangerous work because of
their poison. Though the inhalation and breathing go
along with it, yet it carries many things with it which
can hardly be extracted; nor does it repay the labor and
expenses. Miners name the matter in their own way
indiscriminately and inconsiderately—just as the greedy
alchemists suppose, when men speak of the mercury of bodies,
that it refers to the mercury of metals; or the salts of bodies,
to be a salt like other common salt. Ashes of *Saturn* <note>lead</note> are seen here
appropriately before they rise or go any higher, and
before they turn into silver, for they soon embrace *Antimony*;
the congealed water or coagulated *Saturn*
has the same properties likewise; it is a pure proof for all
metals and grows deep.

Then there is a pure wismuth <note>bismuth</note>, which is coarse and
contains a congealed water of *Saturn*, which is found also
with great profit around metals. When they are reduced to
their first matter, it then ascends into a metallic glass
work, and the ash-work ceases. Chemists in old times, and
now also, made themselves very busy with their salt to
make all bodies potable thereby, having first reduced them
into salts; but here is an *album* <note>white substance</note> out of ashes,
which ashes afford salts, but this is in vain. For ashes are
garments, indicating and pointing to the thing which is
clothed, and the ore clothes itself with it when it is
coming near the day <note>the surface</note>. The nature of *Potabilia* <note>potable substances or medicines</note>
is different; they must be brought into potability, and
this is done in a way completely contrary to how they
go to work. But those who dig for nothing but gold are
served well enough; Nature gives such people these
garments. It gives them the slip before men are aware of
it—I mean the body—therefore, look closely into
bodies.

## CHAP. XX.

### *Of Scobes and metalline water, of the Schlich and lye (Lixivium.)*

Nature in her work must always have an increase and decrease; some things are coming, and others are going. And just as above ground at the melting furnace and at the hammer, metals lose something, so it is underground. But how these subterranean decreases are discerned—which, like crumbs of bread, should be preserved—Nature, being provident, keeps them together also, bringing them to the daylight so that they might be put to good use. And it is known that metal is also under that ground by the *Scobs* <note>dross or filings</note> or alkali which it excretes—namely, the rocks, stones, and flints which sucked nature dry in her work. These are the offal if they are empty of metals; and if some good ore is in it, then that may well be called *Schlich* <note>fine-grained ore slime</note> or sliding, because it slides away from the work; it steals away. Where such sliding is, take notice of it, for it breaks off from the matter in the *officina* <note>workshop</note> wherein metals are in their work and perform their office; and that *Schlich* or sliding is a sure sign that metals are in that place.

So the *Lixivium* <note>leached solution or lye</note> or metallic water is also a sure forerunner of metals; it is always dissolving in the work and loosening something from the metals, in which there is a *Schnede* <note>sharpness or potency</note> and virtue. For as I have said when I mentioned minerals, where there are *fossilia mineralia* <note>mined minerals</note>, there is surely a mine-work. And where such minerals are, they soon dissolve into a water or *lixivium*. This is the difference between the water and the *lixivium*: water affords only the mineral, while the metals provide the flowers <note>essential extracts or sublimates</note> for it; from these comes a *lixivium*. The effect of this water

For if a mineral is desiccated <note>dried out</note>, then it no longer has the strength to run forth, but grows tough and is dried up, sinking into the ground. There are waters in *Hungary* savoring of sulfur and alum, which afford a store of gold and copper; aluminous water in *Misnia* affords silver and copper; the waters in *Bohemia* which taste of saltpeter <note>saliter</note> or iron afford several sorts of metal, except gold. The mines in *Styria* have their special metallic waters and *lixiviums*, besides other excellent mine-works, so much so that the whole mines consist of mere steel, copper, gold, silver, quicksilver, and other things men's hearts may wish for. The salt waters at *Frankenhausen* <note>Franckenhouse</note>—do they not signify that there are curious mine-works there? If the inhabitants took more notice of them, and if they were better known, what gains could they not make from them? ## CHAP. XXI.

### Of *Scoria* and *Exuvium*, of the seed and hull of the seed.

The greatest and final reduction and lessening of metals is cinder, which is very good and harmless. I mean that cinder which metals shed by their *uredines* <note>scorching or burning qualities</note> or soaps. For the *exuvium* <note>husk or outer skin</note> or husk which the coruscation or "by-breathing" leaves behind, and is like a cinder, is another sort of cinder, similar to that which comes from the forges of smiths and smelters; for besides weighing their bodies, they also cleanse them; though the lye purges the minerals and colors, they also purge themselves, as is seen by the cinders which every metal leaves behind in the fire, which, however, are not like this. By this cinder it is also seen that there are metals nearby; for the cinders of minerals, which the coruscation causes, are palpable, known, and visible; knowledgeable miners know this. There is a metallic cinder among the slag <note>OCR reads "flacks"</note>, but it is not recognized, which is why slag is so brittle; otherwise, it would be malleable, or else it could be cast solidly. Some have undertaken that work, but they could not remove these cinders.

Regarding the *Schwaden* <note>vapors or exhalations</note> or husks, these clearly indicate the departure of the seed and the completion of the work of all bodies: for when the husk ceases to work and has no more food—neither from the minerals nor from the bodies—and now the *Folium* <note>leaf</note> has turned into *Spolium* <note>spoils or waste</note>, then it is in the process of separating and breaks the bond between the leaf and the seed, which is called the *Schwaden* or husk. It is an extreme poison; it destroys all that lives, especially breathing things, for its intent is to return there again. Therefore, to whatever place it comes, finding things that move and stir, it destroys them

Where such poison is, be sure that there was silver and metal there, but never in that place where it went forth and turned into another thing; for after the decay of each body and of its leaf, and after the plucking off of a singular husk, the last thing it makes is the fiercest. For there it lies like a *Speculum* <note>mirror</note> upon the water and easily turns into nourishment, which is the reason why it turns so soon to the metals; and so the nourishments are infected contrary to their quality. The reason that it wanders so much up and down is because it contains some of the seed and of the life, and is the hostility: for the seed must have something in which it may lie, and if it is not one of the bodies of the seven metals, then it is such a poison or husk. This is the skin in which it contains itself until the coruscation <note>a flash or vibration of light</note> forces it away, which then is death to it.

## CHAP. XXII.

### Of the shining rod, or of the fire rod.

HE who intends to work with rods must not follow his own fancy, nor bring novelties into mine works based on his own suppositions. For nature does not tolerate being curbed in her order, but men must be regulated according to nature. Concerning the rod, good notice must be taken of the breathing; and this *Lucens virgula* <note>shining rod</note>, or fire-rod, is ordered upon the operative and attractive breathing. For if it were strong, even though it does not ignite, yet it performs its office through and by a heat appropriate to its quality. A great heat in a furnace extinguishes a small heat, light, or fire; the same effect this breathing has upon this rod, which, being ignited and inserted, is put out. No upper air or wind can hurt it, for our upper fire cannot live or burn underground; for if a light or

# VALENTINUS BASILIUS.

If a candle hits against a stone or earth, it is extinguished because it cannot penetrate it. It attracts the nourishment which makes this rod burn and sucks it dry. This is remarkable in the material of this rod: that it has an <term>unctuosity</term> <note>oiliness</note> which does not burn, just as the seed’s husk does, whose poison puts out lights underground in an extraordinary manner. And the <term>breathing</term> <note>metallic exhalation</note> above ground does the same in a peculiar manner. 

Few miners know this fire, and it is the only means by which the <term>inhalation</term> <note>the drawing in of vapors</note> is known. This <term>fire-work</term> <note>chemical or metallurgical operation</note>, thus extracted, is of special use in mines, and then in the subsequent work it is of various use for metals; of which more will be said in its proper place. To detect fire by fire is no small skill, and the subterranean fire can be mastered in no other way. 

Ancient philosophers have written much about it: how superior elements join with the inferior, also suggesting that by means of the middle elements, the energy of the superior and subterranean must be learned. For they are spirits which join the souls above with the grosser bodies below; otherwise, nothing could have any success within the earth. For their operation, there must be a *medium*, a *gluten* <note>glue or binding agent</note>. To tie and bind fire with fire is a strange maxim, but it is a true one; hence comes a reserve of fire which never burns. All three must be together: for the upper is the light fire, the metal is the flame-fire, and the lower is the burning fire. 

We shall know this at the Great Day in the next life, where God will separate the burning from the flame when it flames. Then the burning will consume Hell, and Hell will soon be burned away; nor will there be any clarity, but darkness, because God bestows the light upon His chosen ones, which is neither a flame nor a burning; here on earth, these must stand together. Become acquainted with its friendliness and friendship; turn it to your advantage, for it is hidden away and moves invisibly.

## CHAP. XXIII.

### Concerning the Glowing Rod.

**B**ecause the use of the rod has fallen into abuse among many people, it is nevertheless a fundamental way to know and to bring forth metals, if it is duly and naturally used. It is an undeniable truth, as I mentioned before, that metals breathe, and this breathing is invisible. The best means to learn of it is the rod; this is the reason why I call this rod the "glowing rod," because it reveals and shows the exhalation of metals. This exhalation consists of a fiery heat, and though it casts neither flame nor sparks, yet it is of such a heat that it glows in its own manner and causes the rod to glow, which is an infallible sign that there is a living metal nearby. 

If this rod is to glow more without a flame, a special matter must be used which receives that fire; otherwise, it cannot be done with any utility. Concerning that rod: it is a stick or staff, half an ell <note>approximately 22.5 inches</note> in length, made of hard wood such as oak. To it is fastened the material, just as a torch or link <note>a torch made of pitch and tow</note> is made of pitch or wax. This material must be an unctuous <note>oily or fatty</note> substance, derived from an animal, vegetable, or something else found upon or above the ground. It must not be too strong; otherwise, it sets the breathing on fire. No tallow, wax, pitch, or resin is fit to be used, nor any mineral; otherwise, that breath, mineral, or metal would set its food on fire and consume it. It must be a <term>calx</term> <note>a powdery substance produced by heating or roasting</note> of earth, which catches that heat suddenly and smolders in a heat just as <term>calx</term> does above ground; when moistened, it falls hot from the rod. 

This lime, burnt above ground by the breathing, is good for several uses. But this breathing does not seize upon the unctuosity or mine-wax; otherwise, how could the nourishment of minerals prosper? Miners call it a <term>Spath</term> <note>spar, a crystalline mineral found in veins</note>,

<summary>This chapter describes the "glowing rod," a tool used by miners to detect the invisible, fiery exhalations of metals. The author explains the rod's construction, emphasizing that it must be coated with a specific organic unctuous material and earthy calx to capture the metallic "breathing" without igniting it.</summary>

a true <term>calx</term> <note>a powdery substance produced by roasting or heating</note> of the earth. It is not corroded or seized upon underground because it has too much humidity. Some call it a mine *Mermel* <note>possibly marble or a similar mineral</note> or subterranean *Mermel*, but it is no such thing, because it does not endure the weather above ground; it dries out, and by its breathing is finally kindled.

The huge mountains in *Norway* and the ores in *Swedland* <note>Sweden</note> seize upon it and corrode it; they are full of *Spath* <note>spar</note> or <term>calx</term>, and in their glowing they grow hollow. If they were in a flame, that land would have been consumed long ago. For as soon as that *earth-water* comes forth from this *Spath*, and the desiccating earth comes forth also, then the hidden fire falls into their places.

### C H A P. X X I V.

->Concerning the Leaping Rod<-

While metal is in its purification, so that it neither rises nor moves, then it has its breathing; because it is of a unique condition, it must be discovered and led forth by a unique rod. This rod consists of two sticks held together with both hands. Where there is such a breathing, it lies close upon that rod; no man is able to hold these sticks together if it breathes on them. And if it were a single stick, it would break in two. The inner side where this rod is joined together must be anointed with *Marcafite* <note>marcasite</note>; that breathing draws it downward, just as the magnet has an attractive quality to draw iron. So the breathing of this ore draws the *Marcafite*, because the condition and property of the *Marcafite* is to strengthen the breathing of the purification. There is no better way of refining than to use for each metal its peculiar *Marcafite*. There is a twofold *Marcafite*: one above the *uredines* <note>subterranean vapors or "blasts"</note>, or upper

### CHAP. XXV.

->*Of Furcilla, or of the striking rod.*<-

**A**s man's natural breath goes in and out, so also is the exhalation, and it carries all the rest. A man's breath, when he drinks wine, smells quite a bit of it, or after any other strong-scented food; this carries this breath in and out, and all other scented breaths out of man's body. Even so do I compare this exhalation also with a natural living breath, which carries all the rest that come in the <term>Ferch</term> <note>In historical mining contexts, this refers to the vital spirit or essential "sap" of the earth and minerals</note> and seed. So there is no other striking-rod for it but what is of a year's growth, which otherwise is called a summer-lath; this the miners cut off in the usual way, and so in the Name of God they go. If it strikes, then it is good; if not, then his unlucky hand and misfortune are blamed, which have fallen upon him, and he thinks that his handiworks are not blessed. Thus, out of a false concept, men suppose their own aptness and disposition either hinder or further them therein, not considering God's gift and blessing; and most of them do not know at what or why these rods strike. They wear it under their girdle, or on their hat-band, and keep it with devotion, as their superstition leads them, to get their bread according to their necessity. God has bestowed enough on them, if they knew how to manage their things judiciously.

There is one thing to be noted also: that the several airs or breathings have their several operations, especially upon aerial things. For astronomers appropriate some trees and fruits to the air. The upper air has the upper part of the trees and their fruits; the lower air possesses the root, milks and attracts the juice they yield, because it is their allotted part as well as the upper part is allotted for the

He further notes that different layers of the atmosphere influence different parts of plants, from the roots in the lower air to the fruit in the upper air.</summary>

This sap sinks in due time, being drawn up by degrees by a hot air from below, which is their food, their work, matter, and all aerial things.

Therefore, if you take such a rod, especially of a hazel or of a kray tree <note>Likely referring to a cherry tree, from the German "Kirschbaum"</note>, whose sap is full and which bears a pleasant and sweet fruit, it draws the same downward so that the rod must sink and strike; it will suck out the juice when the rod stands right over its passage. This rod draws the exhalation naturally after the sap so strongly that the rod does bow to the exhalation and to that earth, unless it were so strongly pinned that it could not bow. It does not draw obliquely, and strikes neither at the upper nor the lower, but at the middle part, which is called the wind, and is good neither for the one, nor for the other; neither does it breathe, thrusting together the beams, making their outer rind. According as it is either good or bad, so it hurts or furthers the upper and lower breathing. This distinction must be well observed; the streams and ores run that way also. For this purpose, the tops of almond trees are used, and of such fruit-trees as consist of a mere kernel and a hard shell; for such shells and the ashes of such trees, and all that they have, are very aerial and fiery, which serve best for the metallic work, and the best kindling coals are made of them, and are very light.

# CHAP. XXVI.

## *Of Virgula trepidans; or of the heaving or trembling Rod.*

The exhalations driving the fumes together above and below—you must understand that it requires great skill to distinguish other exhalations from this one. The exhalations of the upper element are jovial <note>Associated with the planet or metal of Jupiter</note>, and the lower exhalations are jovial also; the same is apparent in their work. The wind is also *Jupiter*; he fumes these together, and that exhalation can hardly be detected by the rod. This skill is of a high order. This heaving rod shows the way; it must be made of a metal plate, such as steel, which must be thrust below the damp earth. At the edge of that earth, these exhalations and inhalations come together, and the rod moves and quakes—which it does not do except at such a time. The rays of the inferior and superior bodies cannot be stopped, except where they remain at their work; for in their breathing they have their ascent and descent, and are not restrained in their life's progress unless by the material of this rod. There is a knot on that stick, and it is hollow like a cane. This material of the rod is made of an <term>Electrum</term>, drawn from the best thin plates of <term>Luna</term> and <term>Sol</term>, placed into a glass head. This does not hinder the exhalation, because the exhalation will not be hindered as it might be by a vessel made above ground. Therefore, see how you succeed if you drive it in too deep, or if you do not take material from the lower earth matter, as has been mentioned before.

Regarding the strength you perceived in the striking rod—if there is an aerial exhalation nearby, you see how that rod quakes; for the lower ascends and touches, and the upper descends and touches likewise, which rouses, as it were, the <term>Ferch</term>, or the life and seed of the metal. The like appear-

## CHAP. XXVII.

### Of the falling rod, or of the nether rod.

**T**hat there is a peculiar and vehement moving quality and power in the breathing and exhalation upward and downward, which may not be stopped by any opposition, is apparent in all the plants of the world. Rain and great thick mists may keep the solar shine from us, but nothing can stop this breathing because it strikes directly through; but if it proves weak in its fertility, it is the fault of the mild air. A shot out of a gun, if it is to go a great distance through the air, is cooled. This is the reason why the solar beams do not have the same operation underground as they have above it, and hence also it is that the metallic matter is not as fertile; however, they do what they can: they descend to the metal, where they rouse a fire and preserve it. Thus, the sun's beams must accomplish everything in a magnificent and glorious manner—not only above, to preserve the lights of all the stars with its luster, but also by day to impart daylight to the world and to men. By these beams, it casts a dispersed fire into the earth, which is advantageous for the preservation of subterranean things; they thrust together similar things and bring metals to a compactness so that they may be obtained pure above ground. They rely and lean on them; from this, the rod has its origin.

A striking rod is used; at its fork, a portion of the stick three inches long is left. At the cut end, take out the pith and put superfine *Sol* <note>gold</note> into the halation <note>likely "hollow" or "exhalation"</note> which goes in, pulling the rod toward where the ore is. The reason for this is because the lower breathing of the air is there, and it goes against and draws this breathing, carries it down, and the metal within the rod is pulled downward, as if it would join itself with the rest of that body. Thus the breathings and halations are joined together; the beam must take effect upon the earth in which it is to operate. It brings no rain to the earth, but the vapors of the earth which rise create or cause the rain, snow, and hoar-frost, and bring them back to the earth, soaking it with them. It has the same operation on the subterranean vapors caused by the breathing of the metals within; in their rising, it makes them hea<unclear>vy</unclear>, so that they are able to fall unto *Sol*, helping it in its productive work. Having accomplished the productivity of the work, such metallic rain then also falls on the metal, makes it heavy, moves its *Ferch* <note>vital spirit</note> in the *Lubricum* <note>fluid or slippery state of matter</note>, and draws the rod down even more forcefully than it does a striking rod which does not touch the metal, but merely the breathing, which is not yet ennobled by the halation. This breathing might very well be called a fruitful rain, which thus comfortably refreshes them; it is not a flowing rain, but only a breathing which is made noble by the deep, glittering *Sol*. It is not sufficiently explained when ignorant, simple miners usually say that the Sun, through its influences, works gold, without explaining the manner of it (as I have just mentioned); they themselves may yet go to school and learn their lesson better.

# CHAP. XXVIII.
## Of the superior Rod.

**A**s the planets do not have their fixed and certain revolution in the firmament—for which reason they are called erratic stars—while the lesser ones remain in their fixed stations: metallic bodies are of the same quality; for they do not stick closely to bodies, but rise and fall. For that reason, I compare them to the various motions of the stars above; their body is rolling and changeable, led and tinged by the search and seed, which is for the most part of the Sun’s condition. Planets, along with the Sun, are of a special agreement in *motu & lumine* <note>motion and light</note>, and their body may be said to be *Lubricum & Volatile* <note>fluid and volatile</note>.

This Rod is assigned to planets which attach their bodies to the seeds as long as the great breathing of Sol <note>the Sun</note> holds still and allows himself to be obscured; and he must suffer in giving his luster to the earth through rain, mist, and snow, until he finally breaks through them. Likewise, the bodies are more corruptible in this upper fire than in the lower in their work. Therefore, such a rod must be applied which I call an upper rod, so that the opposite breathing of the reflecting metallic beams (which were received from the Sun and strengthened again) may be returned from the bottom to the *Systeria* <note>likely referring to the planetary or metallic system/source</note>, no matter how high it may be.

This Furco <note>forked rod</note> or Rod is a twisted, tender branch of a Hazel tree, which is hollow for the length of three fingers from that twist on the stem, for its pith must be removed from it, and the mercury of metals must be put in, weighing three barleycorns or grains. This Rod stirs the halation <note>exhalation or vapor</note> from below upward; this is the nature of this Rod, by which it is known that the metal is of a weak body, lacking the influence of the upper planets, being defective of the Solar heat, and not

The planets, with their influence, work more on imperfect metals than on perfect ones. For the Sun does not put a garment on the mercury of the body, but a certain constellation does it, with the rest joining in the work and being found next to it—except for *Sol* <note>the Sun</note> and *Luna* <note>the Moon</note>; these do not meddle with this Rod. Although these are also planets and belong to a perfect breathing, and have their particular rod as others have theirs (which press toward their several works), they are fitted for not aiming at the work's matter, which makes its principle as the mercury of metals.

There must be sufficient instruction to know it. It is thus: a metal is known before it casts any crust, along with how deep the ore lies and how it may be worked upon. If I would know whether there is a sufficiency in that ore, I learn this by the leap of this Rod; if it be a Saturnal <note>lead-based</note> ore, the Rod leaps more—more than the condition of the body of Saturn is. For this Rod does not stir the body, but that which is most prevalent in that body, namely, the mercury of the body; for it yields a good store of it, and may easily put on another garment and be a mercury of metals, and so forth.

If Tin and Bismuth are in that place, the rod, when applied, does not leap as quickly, but more slowly than it does where Saturn is; where a copper or iron ore is, there that rod moves more slowly still. But this must be observed: there is no breathing about metals unless they are underground; for when they are there, then they are in their works—whether they are increasing or decreasing, they cannot be without breathing then. Thus much concerning these Rods.

## CHAP. XXIX.

### *Of resting vapors, or of their sediments.*

Mine-works encounter many natural obstructions, as all other worldly things are subject to. For all sublunary things are subject to decay; the same also happens to metals; they suffer by their cold and hot fires, so that they completely evaporate and expire. When their vapors and fumes grow heavy, they cannot evaporate upward; where such vapors are, miners cannot endure being underground.

In a cellar where new wine or new beer is fermenting, that vapor allows no candle to burn because of the heavy *Kho* <note>a heavy, choking mineral fume</note> or fume, which cannot escape the ground so quickly unless these fumes are thinned in their heaviness. This fallen matter lies close and is like warm meal; I call it *Caries* <note>Latin for decay or rottenness; here referring to a powdery mineral residue</note>, which differs from an *Osteocollum* <note>a mineral deposit, often carbonate of lime, found in the earth</note>, for that is in the rocks naturally and eventually dissolves in a fire. This does not do so, and remains a dry powder; it is heavier than mine-ashes, which are light and dissolve into salts. This meal does not do that, but remains a constant sediment, which is like an unwholesome, stinking vapor, choking the ores and turning them to rottenness, as they are obstructed in their progress above and below.

These fumes are caused when there are hard rocks to which fire must be applied, which causes ill fumes; these, being weakened in their ascent, settle down in a place, grow heavy, and increase; for evil and poisonous things will soon gather into a heap. It is seen in gold: when dissolved in water and allowed to stand for a while, the impure minerals separate from the essence of gold and settle to the bottom. Waste products of metal are soon brought to that state; for being brought to a glow by a small heat, they cast off such a malignant fume that it chokes men immediately.

<summary>This chapter describes the dangerous, heavy vapors and powdery sediments (such as "Caries") that accumulate in mines, often caused by the use of fire on hard rocks. These poisonous fumes settle in low places and can choke miners or corrupt the quality of the ore.</summary>

## CHAP. XXX.

### Of *weather salt, halitus Melusus.*

**T**he malignant breathing, as it is generally called, is a pestilent salt which endangers not only the miners, but hurts the ores also; for it allays or makes the breathings fall which are in the ore, thereby obstructing them in their natural working. The manner in which these breathings settle is worthy of note. It may be compared to an eclipse; though the beams which ascend keep their course, yet the breathing stays them and will not let them move about that place. Being in that state and condition, its <term>Ferch</term> <note>a term for the vital spirit or life-force within a mineral</note> and seed part; at last, through its sides or walls, it allows fluid ores to come in. From this originates such fluid ores, of which there are several sorts. But this is to be noted: that *weather* is called *weather* because it is not of such clear air as it is above ground with us; it always carries something with it which is grosser and more hurtful to man than the air above with us. For men are not commanded to dwell and live underground, but if anyone's employment lies that way, he must be content to do so and commit his condition to God Almighty, because he must endure and breathe in ill fumes and contract mine-diseases.

When this subterranean air grows heavy because of the fumes and water-stone <note>likely a mineral deposit or concretion that blocks ventilation</note> which block and corrupt the cross-passages, it is then called a *weather-salt*. This is apparent when a burning candle is brought in; these heavy fumes quench it like water, and at last, they choke the men also. Therefore, when that is seen and felt, let miners come out again, for they can do no good there.

# CHAP. XXXI.

## *Of Cos metallicus, or stone-salt.*

The air, being of one piece of pure earth, at last turns into a stone. There are several sorts of stones, just as there are several bodies in the earth, though all of them are but an earth. The longer the matter lies, the harder and fatter it will be. Such stones cannot be moved or lifted, unless by fire, which seizes the earth and consumes it, turning it into a terrene firmament <note>a terrestrial or earthy solid structure</note>, just as there are scales on fish and bones in beasts. These also the Lord will take in His good time with fire. This moved the ancients to consume their carcasses with fire; though flesh soon turns to rottenness underground, yet the bones do not consume so soon; the fire consumes them after they are turned to earth-ashes.

This salt is harmful to metals, taking from them their working; without it <note>the ability to "work" or process naturally</note> they cannot continue in their life, but must expire and die, because nothing can penetrate it. The difference between the stone-salt and the stone-meal is this: the salt lifts itself when the stone grows to a hardness; what formerly was a precious stone that the air could penetrate now begins to turn into a horn-stone <note>a tough, siliceous mineral</note>; when this meal comes to the height of its age, it turns to dust.

Here it is necessary to relate the difference between the lower and upper stone, which is partly obstructive and partly a furtherance to the mine-work; they may increase underground, but in the afterwork <note>the processing of minerals after they have been extracted</note> no stone is good, because they have no nutriment, and therefore must perish and be famished for want of food.

<summary>This chapter describes the formation of stones and stone-salt from subterranean air and earth, explaining how fire transforms these substances into solid structures. It also details the differences between stone-salt and stone-meal and their respective impacts on the vitality of metals and mining operations.</summary>

<keywords>Cos metallicus, stone-salt, stone-meal, horn-stone, mine-work, alchemical geology, earth-ashes, terrene firmament</keywords>

->VALENTINUS BASILIUS.<-

## CHAP. XXXII.

### *Of the subterranean pools.*

There is a very difficult situation that affects fissures, passages, and structures, called underground water pools. For it is an undeniable truth that underground fissures and passages have their waters—both the day-water and the night-water—which, if once opened and made passable, prove to be the utter ruin of mine works. Therefore, my advice is not to make passages navigable to reach the ores. It is just as if someone wanted to dig a man’s heart out of his body and began digging at the pulse, continuing all the way to the heart; would that not be a horrific butchery? There is a nearer path to the well; what need is there to take such a roundabout way? There is a twofold water underground: the day-water and the ground-water. The day-water is not harmful to the structures but rather helpful, soaking away many things and often removing the ground-water from the stages. Let the ground-water be kept out as much as possible, for it causes harm if it cannot drain away; nor should it be allowed to spring up from below out of its cataracts <note>deep outlets or floodgates</note>. Keep out the fire also; for both are harmful to metals and to all things in the world if they are allowed to dominate.

<meta>catchword: CHAP.</meta>

<summary>Basilius warns against the dangers of underground water pools and improper drainage in mining, advising direct paths to ore rather than watery channels. He distinguishes between helpful "day-water" and harmful "ground-water," emphasizing that both water and fire must be controlled to prevent the destruction of mine works.</summary>

<keywords>mining, subterranean pools, day-water, ground-water, drainage, mine works, cataracts, resource management</keywords>

## CHAP. XXXIII.

### Of *Aurum metallicum*; of the metallic gold, or of the *Metallic-bed*.

There is another fatness underground where metals grow; it is neither the shell of the seed, nor the stone *Oleum Petroleum* or *Naphtha*, but is like a *uredo* <note>a burning or blistering substance</note> or *Mine-soap*. It differs much from soap; soap does not burn because it contains a hidden sulfur—not a combustible one, but an incombustible one. This is the reason why it does not burn in a flame, nor does it affect anything else but only the pure metal. If that substance did not remain with it, the metal could move neither into ascension nor descension; it consumes first of all in the metal, just as grease does in animals. This fatness is not far from the ore; when it is predominant, it consumes the metal entirely and evaporates. Oil has that quality that if put on the top of a drink, whatever it may be, it keeps in its strength and coolness; this fatness shuts up the ore's body so that no breathing can pass. Fatness has a great affinity with iron and is one of its closest relatives, about which great volumes could be written.

In the County of *Schwartzburg*, at *Wackersberg*, there is a store of such fatness; it looks like quicksilver, and floats and stains red like *Bole* <note>a type of colored clay</note>. This color it took hold of, and it may be extracted from it. Some call it a Mercurial body or a *Tin-glass* <note>Bismuth</note>, *Lead-glass*, *Wismuth* or *Antimony*. It affects all of those, and when this fatness does not turn into a fatness of such metals where the seed is predominant, then it turns into a volatility and into a great robber. This fatness is a greasy earth, glittering like an ointment, with a red and brown glow,

<summary>Basilius describes a subterranean "fatness" or mineral grease that is essential to the formation of metals but can also consume them if it becomes too volatile. He identifies this substance as being related to iron and various mineral glasses found in regions like Schwartzburg.</summary>

<keywords>Aurum metallicum, metallic fatness, Mine-soap, Schwartzburg, Wackersberg, mineralogy, metallurgy, Bismuth, Wismuth, Antimony, iron formation</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of metallic fatness and its locations, then begins a new chapter on metallic streams or "fluxes."</meta>

VALENTINUS BASILIUS.

as if it were *quicksilver* or beaten *talc*, or glass strewn into it. In the rocks of *Bohemia* and *Transylvania*, there is a great store of it at *Goslar* and at *Slackenwald*, just as there are quicksilver or lead ores; many of them are found here and there.

---

### CHAP. XXXIV.

->*Of Metallic streams.*<-

When *ferch* <note>a term likely referring to a type of mineral sediment or "barren" rock</note> and seed must part from their work by reason of the sediments—whatever they may be—and do not expire naturally, then the ores turn to stones which miners call *fluxes*. Although they do not know where they come from, nothing can be made out of them; they are fusible or fluid when melted in the fire, but nothing can be introduced into them because they are not porous, and no air can enter them, which would make them more noble. It is strange in nature that if any good thing is driven out of a body, it will not return there; for if life has left a man, the body receives it no more, though these are things possible for God alone. My intent here is not to write of miraculous things, but only of natural things; I set aside the former. It is admirable that the body of dead metals is so beautiful, whereas other bodies which are dead consume away to nothing. Metals also undergo corruption, but over a long time; their death is like any glass, which keeps its color, especially if it was from a *marcasite*. From this, the colors of *marcasites* are learned, for green, blue, and white fluxes are found within them, just as metallic flowers have been, which are generated from three bodies.

<meta>catchword: CHAP.</meta>

<summary>Basilius Valentinus describes the geographical locations of metallic minerals and introduces the nature of "fluxes," which are non-porous stones formed when mineral development is interrupted. He observes that unlike biological bodies, the "dead" bodies of metals and marcasites can retain their beauty and vibrant colors through a glass-like state.</summary>

<keywords>Bohemia, Transylvania, Goslar, Slackenwald, fluxes, mineralogy, marcasite, metallic flowers, ferch, alchemy</keywords>

## CHAP. XXXV.

### Of Creta, Chalk, or stone-meal.

We see in our air that no fume or wind rises in vain; it dissolves again into one thing or another, and many meteors gather there. The earth has similar meteors underground. For the fume that rises from the fire-exhalation of the ore or of the metal produces the stone-meal (*Creta*) wherever it falls or lands; it grinds more and increases abundantly, containing a dangerous salt that harms the places where metals are located—especially when they are in their development <note>originally "ascension"</note>, hindering their color. This is apparent in the flat at *Mansfield*, where it lies between the spokes <note>likely referring to the veins or radial formations of the ore</note> of the ores and can hardly be removed from there; it robs and consumes *Folium* and *Spolium*. The stone-meal creates a *Kuff* when mixed with stone-marrow, turning it into a kind of marble called *Potstone* or *doblit* (a double stone); it is dark and very firm, and it strikes fire, being composed mostly of fire.

The <term>Talks</term> <note>talc</note> belong here as well, but since I intend to mention them elsewhere, I will pass over them for now. However, they are also a type of meal, though they differ from others because they incline more toward a cold fire, in which they melt like snow. While others dissolve sooner into water than into meal, this turns sooner into meal than into water. If it consists of less matter than it contains of stone-marrow, it produces a beautiful ice or crystal called *Vitrum Alexandrinum*, or *Mary's Ice*. This cannot be mastered in hot fires, but it melts in cold fires and is very harmful to metals; indeed, because of it, mining works fall into ruin, as happened at *Stolberg*.

<summary>Basil Valentine describes "stone-meal" (chalk) as an underground byproduct of metallic fumes that harms ore development and combines with stone-marrow to form hard minerals like potstone. He also discusses the destructive properties of talc and "Mary's Ice" in mining operations.</summary>

<keywords>stone-meal, Creta, chalk, Mansfield, Potstone, doblit, Talc, Mary's Ice, Vitrum Alexandrinum, Stolberg, mineralogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural forces and mineral formation, moving from the impact of stone-meal to the role of air and "the blast" in subterranean processes.</meta>

### CHAP. XXXVI.

->*Of Spiro, or of the Blast.*<-

The *Spiro*, or blast, is an instrument which brings to order the weather or obstructed air; otherwise, everything would turn to stone where it is, and would remain at a standstill there if the lower fire should enter instead of the air. It dries things out <note>originally "exiccateth"</note>, though it does not kindle them; if a piece is beaten off of it, then it appears so, and this piece which flies aside in this way gives us to understand how it makes the stone, and how Nature frames the ore and metal. However, jewels and precious stones come from another origin, out of sweet waters.

In this instrument, fire and air dwell together, which take their power and matter from the malignant weather, where they consume all heavy matters through fire, enlightening the remaining matter it has. Make that *Spiro*, or blast, into a ball of copper the size of a head; solder it bright and light, and let no air get into it, but leave a small hole where a needle may enter, drawing in the water which must be purposely prepared and set for it. There must be a pan of coals at hand, which must be kindled, and the ball laid into it; turn the little hole toward the coal fire, and it will blow the fire forcibly. When this is done, it grows hot and makes the water boil in the ball, which fumes and carries it forth with great fierceness, blowing strongly on the coals. In this way, it maintains the fire by breathing strongly, in the manner of a pair of bellows driven from the outside. By this, several good things are achieved, and the nature of this ball is that it shows what may be done above ground with such a thing; no use can be made of it beyond that place, because Nature herself has such a blast for her fire.

<summary>Basil Valentine describes the "Spiro" or "blast," an experimental copper ball that uses boiling water to create a bellows-like effect, illustrating how nature uses air and fire to form minerals.</summary>
<keywords>Spiro, blast, mineral formation, copper ball, bellows, subterranean fire, precious stones</keywords>

## CHAP. XXXVII.

### Of *Pulsa*, or of the *Break-stuff*, or *brittle matter*.

This salt is usually generated by a malignant fume, which the mine-fire produces; and when the stones are very hard, then a wood fire must be made where the fume is drawn to the stone-fire and grows thick. If the fumes of *Succinum* <note>amber</note> and of other things are joined, it then turns to such poison that the ore must be aided, otherwise it perishes, for that fume lies on the ore as sooty <note>originally "fuliginous"</note> *Kobolt* <note>cobalt</note>, which corrodes and consumes the ore. There, a ball must be used, which is round and hollow, having a hole of a size into which a quill may enter; it must be so tight that no air may either enter or exit. This ball must be filled with gunpowder; cover it with cotton wool boiled in saltpeter, then dip it in melted pitch which is mixed with some sulfur. Kindle that ball and let it go down in a box, or fling it into a *stolln* <note>a horizontal mine gallery or adit</note> or chamber; when the ball flies apart, it expels that fume, not only by the smoke but with the blast or report the gunpowder makes. Such a ball may also be applied to water and be sunk in an instrument under water where troublesome fishes are feared; its explosion will kill the fishes that are there. There is a paste which gives no report but only burns, and destroys and heaves this salt; but be careful what paste you make use of, and have regard for the upper scaffolds, whether they be old or new, so that they are not damaged <note>originally "imbezled"</note>, and your paste must be mixed so that it does no harm.

### CHAP. XXXVIII.
### *Of Clathrum, or of the blank fire.*

This fire needs nothing for its fuel; it shines in darkness and is a special fire for mine-works. If applied, it pays for its own costs more than the expenses spent upon *talc* <note>originally "Talk"</note> or *bismuth* <note>originally "Bromith"</note> work. Since oil may be had cheaply enough in some places, it casts no smoke and destroys fumes. It is placed into a glass ball, which is then put into a basket to keep it safe from water and sand, providing a light for the workmen. 

Miners ought to know how to enter their ground for the height or metalline *Speculum* <note>a mirror or diagnostic instrument</note>, which is a unique manual instrument. Because the metalline breathings and after-exhalations join and come together, they require special instruments by which they may be known; for where these join and the diurnal <note>daytime</note> breathing is predominant, it exhales by day and shines out of the earth. Miners call this a "metalline breathing"; it is true that it is so, but they leave something out, so it is only half a breathing. If the after-breathing is predominant, then it appears by that *Speculum* and light through which it makes itself known. 

Nature is at work with something, and such a metal is at hand; metals do shine (just as rotten wood does), even though it does not appear so to our eyes. By day they are not at rest as long as they are working, but there must be a reflection of their work, which is this light. It casts no beams as daylight or rotten wood does; by night, it receives a beam from such a dark or dusky shadowed light.

Fair and curious breathings are seen within it, and that "light of darkness" is a light you may see by. He who is five or six yards distant from it cannot see it, and neither can you [from a distance], for it is such a light as is in the eyes of cats, dogs, and wolves, which can see you even though you cannot see them.

<meta>catchword: T</meta>

<summary>This chapter describes "Clathrum" or "blank fire," a smokeless and fuel-free light source used in mines that reveals the presence of metals through their "breathings." The light functions similarly to the reflective eyes of nocturnal animals and requires a special "Speculum" for proper observation of mineral vapors.</summary>

<keywords>Clathrum, blank fire, mining technology, metalline breathings, Speculum, mineral exhalations, bioluminescence, talc, bismuth</keywords>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>his last Will and Testament.</header>

...them, for there is a light at night as well as by day, which is apparent in these bodies, which receive their light from this nocturnal light. For if that power were in themselves, they would emit beams, which they do not; and experience proves it, that there is a subterranean *ignis dispersus* <note>Latin: scattered fire</note>, a scattered fire.

This light is twofold: the first gives light, being thus prepared in a ball of certain fishes or worms, of juices of herbs, and saps of wood, being distilled, and the distilled water being put into it. Take a pure crystalline glass; it casts a remarkable light underground if mercurial water is put into it. It intensifies the waters made of worms and of woods very highly in this darkness, which is called the Light. It may be done and used also by day, but much better in subterranean darkness, in which the fire lies hidden and must be roused and awakened by such material and instrumental fires.

The second light is the *Speculum* <note>mirror or diagnostic instrument</note>, which receives that light and gives an indication of such hot or cold fires, which not every common laborer or miner understands; for as it shines in the *Speculum*, so this fire kindles, and it is the ore <note>originally "oar"</note>. In a man's body they are discerned well enough, from which the diseases have their various names, but they are not investigated. The difference between the ball and the *Speculum* is the same as that which is above ground: I can view all the members of my body, but not my face; I can behold the light, but what the sun of this light is, which provides the luster to it, I cannot behold or discern.

## CHAPTER XXXIX.

### *Of the Gluten, or Mine-glue.*

The best help and remedy which may be applied to subterranean pools are wells; for where these break forth,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on managing water in mines, specifically the use of "Gluten" (mine-glue) to seal off unwanted water sources, and introduces a method for corroding rock using a substance called "TRUTA."</meta>

They carry that water away; there is no better or nearer way than the Gluten <note>a type of mine sealant or glue</note> to dam up or keep out the day-water <note>surface water</note>, so that it does not run any farther. This damming has great utility; it not only makes the water slimy and tough, but it drives it backward so that it may be used for some other outlet, and be rid of in that place where it is worthless and merely obstructive.

If the day-water is thus stopped by a Gluten so that it cannot run and gather at the sink <note>a sump or collection pit</note>, then that ground-water may soon be drawn away at the sink. The deeper the sinks are cleansed, the more these ground-waters or springs are diverted, and at last they are also turned into day-waters, or may be dammed up and made to run another way where they may not be obstructive to the scaffolds <note>structural supports in the mine</note>. And where mill-drives are not located at those same places, the Gluten may be used; then the scaffolds and structures in the passages, clefts, and mines may be seen. The dams and the Gluten are the best helps for this.

# CHAP. XL.

## Of **TRUTA**, *or of a Paste to corrode the stone through, or by eating through.*

There is almost nothing which is a greater hindrance to mine-works than water is. In places where the Gluten is not sufficient to keep it out, and where it is shut in and must be drawn away with lower buildings—such as with a *Stoln* <note>an adit or drainage tunnel</note>, or beams and pipes—it is a huge and dangerous task to make these breakthroughs <note>originally "thorow-breathings"</note> good and lasting. It costs many men's lives, and great expenses must be incurred; therefore, ways and means must be thought of to make ways through with burning, to create such a fire which corrodes the rock and grinds the stone, eating it

<summary>This page explains how to use mine sealant (Gluten) to divert surface and ground water away from mine workings and introduces the challenge of creating drainage tunnels, suggesting the use of fire or corrosive pastes like TRUTA to break through rock.</summary>

<keywords>mining engineering, Gluten, day-water, ground-water, drainage, Stoln, TRUTA, fire-setting, mine ventilation, water management</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for using "Gluten" and "TRUTA" (a corrosive paste) to create drainage holes through solid rock to clear flooded mines.</meta>

small and thin, so that the water may get through and run away, so that the miners may not lose their lives in that water, as usually happens to them in such works. This fire corrodes great stones in running waters or rivers; it is a corroding fire. A Gluten <note>a chemical paste or sealant</note> is made, which is lined or covered with combustibles and poured or cast down through a channel or pipe protected from water. So that the Gluten may be effective, even though it be many fathoms deep under water, it still corrodes further, gathering strength from that; it eats away, burns, and presses even lower. It does not smoke, being a running corrosive fire.

There are some saps and gums which, if boiled to a hardness and mingled with unslaked lime, kindle and burn so strongly that they corrode the rock and make a hole in it as large and as deep as you wish to have it, so that the water must sink away. There must be set a pipe of wood or of other materials as deep as the water rose, and it must be set and sunk to the very bottom. This Gluten, paste, or substance must be put into it; let the hole of the pipe be closed with pitch to keep out the water. Make small bullets of this paste and kindle them; it eats down even out at the *Stoll* <note>an adit or drainage tunnel</note>, or the end of the beams. The size of the hole must match the pipe's mouth below, which must be equally as wide as that above. When the pipe is cleared and a way is made for the water to run out at the hole, then all that underground water-pool will sink away and clear the chambers below. This is a refined skill for breaking through rocks, if well-contrived and well-ordered with exact manual procedures.

### CHAP. XLI.

->Of the Traha, or heaving materials used instead of a dray or sled.<-

It is known that breathing, exhalation, and the weather support all things, both artificial and natural. It is apparent in great buildings that things exposed to the weather cannot last if neither water nor wind is kept back; the extensive repairs in such structures prove this. 

There is a place in *Zips* or *Sepusium*, called the *Tobeschaw*, where solid steel rises by day, and in that place there is no mine of steel, and no instrument can chip any scale from it; but lying in the weather for one winter and summer, it gains a scale two fingers thick. Thus it is apparent that the weather also heaves a *stoll* <note>an adit or mine tunnel</note>, or the great beam or metallic body; why should it not lift and heave a stone? This appears further at the falling down of great snowballs from the mountains around *Salzburg* and in *Styria*, where great pieces of rocks as big as a house fall down with such snowballs, which heat and cold have thus corroded and loosened. *Hannibal*, making the *Alps* passable for his army, poured warmed vinegar on the rocks, by which he made them so brittle that they could soon be worked through; oil does the same if well prepared. Acetums <note>acidic solutions or vinegars</note> made from the fermentation of wine, beer, or fruits are precious for such purposes. Cistern waters may be turned into acetums if boiled with honey after first being made warm; this drives back the fire which is in stones, for there are commonly *Horn-stones* and *Fire-stones* which are made brittle by such means. There is also a *Petroleum* made, so that you need no salad oil nor any other; no, not even *Naphtha* drawn from *Osteinmark* or calcined flints, such water-acetums being

It stands upon natural reason that such stones must be dealt with in this manner; for behold the *Gluten* <note>a binding or sticky substance</note> and *Aquafort* <note>aqua fortis, or nitric acid</note>, of what efficacy are these? Do they not corrode the *Pumice*-stone like beeswax, and the *Top*-stone <note>possibly a reference to topaz or a specific surface rock</note> like a marble of various colors? Consider well the white marble and the sliding sand in which the pumice is found; you will find what manner of *lixiviums* <note>solutions prepared by leaching or lye</note> may be boiled from them.

### CHAP. XLII.
->Of the frost in Mine-works.<-

The greatest troubles that miners are put to is to pull and draw up all the filth and stones that are worthless out of the mine-works, so that a way may be made to go deeper. Above ground they call it a heap of rubbish. It costs no great matter to cleanse, dissolve, and clear away this rubbish with corrosive waters; it costs little, if rightly managed and handled, to dissolve first the lightest things; these being cleared away, the rest may easily be removed. That earth underground must not be looked upon as the earth is above with us, adorned with grass, for underground there is the least amount of earth; there is a mixture of all manner of things, such as salts, juices, minerals, and stones. The smallest part is earth, and yet that part is the noblest; for out of it are made all manner of metallic bodies. There are sharper things, all of which must not be used at once; and [the work] must be effected with these when that which is above cannot be applied to that which is beneath. Juices also are easy in their uses for corroding and making things brittle. Sulphur alone performs the work, which is a poison to juices and saps. Miners and those who are employed in such works must have knowledge of such things, and exercise

He also describes the composition of the deep earth as a mixture of salts and minerals that serve as the foundation for metallic bodies.</summary>
<keywords>mining, aqua fortis, corrosive waters, lixiviums, metallic bodies, sulphur, mineral juices, rock removal</keywords>
